Dirichlet problem on perturbed conical domains via converging generalized power series
Martin Costabel, Matteo Dalla Riva, Monique Dauge, Paolo Musolino

Abstract
. We consider the Poisson equation with homogeneous Dirichlet conditions in a family of domains in ℝ n {\mathbb{R}}^{n} indexed by a small parameter ε \varepsilon . The domains depend on ε \varepsilon only within a ball of radius proportional to ε \varepsilon and, as ε \varepsilon tends to zero, they converge in a self-similar way to a domain with a conical boundary singularity. We construct an expansion of the solution as a series of fractional powers of ε \varepsilon , and prove that it is not just an asymptotic expansion as ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 , but that, for small values of ε \varepsilon , it converges normally in the Sobolev space H 1 H^{1} . The phenomenon that solutions to boundary value problems on singularly perturbed domains may have convergent expansions is the subject of the Functional Analytic Approach by Lanza de Cristoforis and his collaborators. This approach was originally adopted to study small holes shrinking to interior points of a smooth domain and heavily relies on integral representations obtained through layer potentials. To relax all regularity assumptions, we forgo boundary layer potentials and instead exploit expansions in terms of eigenfunctions of the Laplace-Beltrami operator on the intersection of the cone with the unit sphere. Our analysis is based on a two-scale cross-cutoff ansatz for the solution. Specifically, we write the solution as a sum of a function in the slow variable multiplied by a cutoff function depending on the fast variable, plus a function in the fast variable multiplied by a cutoff function depending on the slow variable. While the cutoffs are considered fixed, the two unknown functions are solutions to a 2 × 2 2\times 2 system of partial differential equations that depend on ε \varepsilon in a way that can be analyzed in the framework of generalized power series when the right-hand side of the Poisson equation vanishes in a neighborhood of the perturbation. In this paper, we concentrate on this case. The treatment of more general right-hand sides requires a supplementary layer in the analysis and is postponed to a forthcoming paper.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification

35B25,
35C20,
35J05,
41A58

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Aim

Consider a bounded domain
\Omega
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that coincides near the origin with a cone
\Gamma
. Assume that
\varepsilon
is a small positive parameter and that the domain is perturbed in a neighborhood of size
\varepsilon
near the vertex of the cone. The perturbed domain is thought of as a member of a family of domains
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, where, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, the perturbation shrinks to the vertex point in a self-similar way. The goal is to compare the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of a boundary value problem (BVP) on
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
with the solution
u
on the unperturbed domain
\Omega
.

This type of singular perturbation problem is typically addressed with methods of asymptotic analysis, as shown in the books by Maz’ya, Nazarov, and Plamenevskij
[
32
,
33
]
(see especially
[
32
, Chapter 4]
). In particular, there is a vast literature on problems where a small perturbation is located in the interior of a domain, a situation which corresponds, in our setting, to the case where the cone
\Gamma
coincides with the whole space. For such problems, there exists a variety of methods that yield asymptotic expansions, mostly stemming from the method of Matched Asymptotic Expansions
[
21
]
and its variants.

Lanza de Cristoforis
[
27
]
observed that for these interior perturbations, the dependence of
u_{\varepsilon}
on
\varepsilon
can often be described by an analytic function defined in a neighborhood of
\varepsilon=0
. Thus, not only by asymptotic approximations but by
convergent
power series of
\varepsilon
. To prove this, he first represented the solution using layer potentials supported on boundary components associated with different scales and then transformed the singularly perturbed BVP into a system of equations for which the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
corresponds to a
regular
perturbation. These few lines synthesize the core idea of the method he called the “Functional Analytic Approach” (FAA), which has since been generalized in many directions, as overviewed in
[
11
]
. In particular, in our previous paper
[
6
]
, the FAA was applied to a problem in a perturbed two-dimensional corner domain. By using a conformal mapping, we reduced the problem to one in a domain with interior holes, which was then analyzed using the standard toolkit of the FAA.

In the present paper, we introduce a generalization of the FAA that overcomes several of the drawbacks found in
[
6
]
. Specifically, we do not use conformal mappings, making our new method applicable in any dimension
n\geq 2
(and not just for
n=2
), and we do not use boundary integral representations, which allows us to greatly reduce the regularity requirements for the domains and the perturbations. Instead, we exploit expansions on a basis of homogeneous harmonic functions in the cone that are related to the eigenfunctions of Laplace-Beltrami operator of the cone’s base (its intersection with the unit sphere). As a result, we don’t need any regularity assumptions on the base of the cone or the perturbations, and we can analyze a large variety of perturbations, such as cracks, several cones touching at their tips, holes tangent to the boundary, and “the rounding of the corner,” which is the approximation of the corner domain by smooth domains. Many of these were excluded in
[
6
]
. The rounding of the corner, for example, could not be treated because it led to non-Lipschitz boundary components. The price to pay for this broader generality is that, for now, we only consider homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions, the Laplace operator, and right-hand sides that vanish in a neighborhood of the tip of the cone.

Depending on the specific problem under consideration, the application of the FAA yields different analyticity properties of the solution. In simpler cases, the solution depends analytically on
\varepsilon
, whereas in more complex scenarios, a richer analytic structure is required, involving multiple scales which can include fractional powers of
\varepsilon
or even
1/\log\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon\log\varepsilon
, and so on.

For the problem analyzed in this paper, particularly when
n\geq 3
, we encounter a situation where an infinite number of scales with fractional powers are necessary. These powers correspond to the eigenvalues of the Laplace-Beltrami operator on the base of the cone. The solutions
u_{\varepsilon}
are then expressed in terms of convergent generalized power series of
\varepsilon
. This is a new level of generality compared to previous works. The underlying algebraic framework is based on the theory of generalized power series developed by Hahn and others
[
18
]
, which we will recall and adapt to our purposes.

1.2.
State of the art

Understanding how the solution of a BVP depends on perturbations of the domain finds applications in various theoretical and practical scenarios. For instance, it is relevant in the study of inverse problems (e.g., Ammari and Kang
[
2
]
), topological optimization (e.g., Novotny and Sokołowski
[
37
]
), and composite materials (e.g., Movchan, Movchan, and Poulton
[
35
]
). One common setting is that of singular perturbations, where a problem is defined on a regular domain, but as a positive parameter
\varepsilon
tends to zero, some regularity is lost or the topology changes. Examples include domains with shrinking holes or rounded corners becoming sharp (the “rounding of a corner” mentioned above). The prevailing approach in the literature is that of asymptotic analysis, which employs techniques like Matched Asymptotic Expansions (as in Il’in
[
20
,
21
]
) or Multiscale Approximation Methods (like the Compound Approximation Method of Maz’ya, Nazarov, and Plamenevskij
[
32
,
33
]
and Kozlov, Maz’ya, and Movchan
[
24
]
). In both cases, an iterative algorithm is used to obtain asymptotic approximations of the solution as
\varepsilon
tends to zero. The results are usually presented as a finite sum of functions of
\varepsilon
plus a remainder that tends to zero as
\varepsilon\to 0
. The vanishing order of the remainder is known and increases when more terms are considered in the expansion. However, there is, at least in general, little information about the size of the remainders for a fixed positive value of
\varepsilon
. We may not even know if, for
\epsilon
fixed, the remainders converge to a limit when the number of terms goes to infinity. As a consequence, we cannot usually claim the convergence of the expansion for small positive values of
\varepsilon
. For many classical singular perturbation problems, where not the domain but the coefficients of the differential operator depend on
\varepsilon
, the series do indeed not converge (see
[
38
]
for examples).

The FAA developed by Lanza de Cristoforis addresses the convergence issue by showing that solutions to perturbed BVPs can often be expressed in terms of analytic functions of the perturbation parameter
\varepsilon
. In these situations, the asymptotic expansions are, in fact, convergent power series.

So far, the strategy of the FAA consists in reducing the problem to the boundary using integral equations. This leads to functional equations of the form

(1.1)

{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]({\mathcal{U}})={\mathcal{F}}\,,

where
{\mathcal{M}}
is a map defined on a (possibly one-sided) neighborhood of
\varepsilon=0
with values in a suitable space of operators and such that
{\mathcal{M}}[0]
is the limit of
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
as
\varepsilon\to 0
and is invertible.
The solution
{\mathcal{U}}
of this system typically consists of several components associated with geometric objects of different scales (connected components of the boundary in the integral equation method) that can be brought to fixed sizes by rescaling. As
\varepsilon\to 0
, the different scales become uncoupled, and then
{\mathcal{M}}[0]
corresponds to the simultaneous solution of several possibly independent problems, one of which is the original boundary value problem on the unperturbed domain. In the case of a perturbation by small holes of size
\varepsilon
, the additional problem is formulated on the “perturbation pattern”
{\mathsf{P}}
, the unbounded exterior of the small holes rescaled to size one. This extension of the space of solutions is the mechanism that allows the embedding of the original singular perturbation problem in a regular perturbation problem.

The dependence of the BVP solution on
\varepsilon
can be recovered from the dependence of
{\mathcal{U}}
on
\varepsilon
. In the ideal scenario,
{\mathcal{M}}
is analytic in
\varepsilon
and, in particular, it can be written as a power series of
\varepsilon
that converges for
\varepsilon
close to
0
. Since the inverse of a convergent power series is still a convergent power series, we can then deduce from (
1.1
) an analyticity result for
{\mathcal{U}}
.

In other applications, we do not have analyticity on
\varepsilon
, but still, we might represent the solution for
\varepsilon
in a one-sided neighborhood
[0,\varepsilon_{0})
of
0
in terms of analytic functions of several variables evaluated at a vector whose entries are given by certain elementary functions of
\varepsilon
. The presence of analytic functions of several variables arises in various works, for example in
[
28
]
, where the two-dimensional Dirichlet problem forces the introduction of a scale of
1/\log\varepsilon
alongside
\varepsilon
, or in
[
12
,
13
]
, where boundary value problems in a domain with moderately close holes are studied
and the size of the holes and their distance are defined by small parameters that may be of different size. Similar results are obtained for holes approaching the outer boundary of a domain, as
[
3
,
4
]
in collaboration with Bonnaillie-Noël and Dambrine.

From the beginning, potential theoretic methods play a crucial role in the application of the FAA. The initial papers employing the FAA were dedicated to studying the Riemann map in planar perforated domains (cf.
[
27
]
). These works explored both singular and regular perturbations by analyzing specific properties of Cauchy and Cauchy-like integral operators. Subsequently, a similar analysis was extended to harmonic layer potentials (see Lanza de Cristoforis and Rossi
[
29
]
), enabling the study of perturbation problems for the Laplace and Poisson equations. For instance, in
[
28
]
, Lanza de Cristoforis considered a Dirichlet problem for the Laplace equation in a domain with a small hole.

Over time, the FAA has been utilized to handle various boundary conditions and different differential operators, all within the established framework of standard potential theory. Noteworthy extensions include applications to the Lamé equations
[
10
]
, Stokes flow
[
9
]
, and the Helmholtz equation
[
1
]
.

Nowadays, the FAA is no longer the only method to obtain analyticity results of this kind. Other approaches have been proposed, still relying on potential theory and properties of related integral operators. For instance, Henríquez and Schwab
[
19
]
use complex analysis methods to prove the “shape holomorphy” of certain integral operators, leading to a (complex) analyticity result for the solution of a BVP in a regularly perturbed two-dimensional domain. Additionally, Feppon and Ammari
[
17
]
address the Dirichlet problem in a domain with a small hole using an approach comparable to the FAA, but with a specific focus on computational efficiency.

However, in some cases, one may not expect analytic dependence on the perturbation parameter
\varepsilon
, or even joint analytic dependence on
\varepsilon
and elementary functions of
\varepsilon
. One example is presented in our previous paper
[
6
]
. In
[
6
]
, we applied the FAA to study a Dirichlet problem in a polygonal domain in the plane with holes that shrink to a corner point in a self-similar manner. There the “natural” scale is a fractional power of
\varepsilon
whose exponent depends on the opening angle of the corner. When the right-hand side in the Poisson equation is not vanishing in a neighborhood of the corner, a phenomenon of small denominators appears: The integer powers of
\varepsilon
coming from the Taylor expansion of the right-hand side may enter into resonance with (multiples of) the natural scale. In this case, the convergence of the expansion of the solution can only be guaranteed by regrouping certain terms into packets of functions that scale according to different homogeneities and are themselves not homogeneous. The expansion does then not correspond to an analytic function of several variables applied to different powers of
\varepsilon
.
In domains perturbed near higher-dimensional conical points, which is the subject of the present paper, the same difficulty appears with higher complexity, and therefore we avoid it for the time being, by assuming that the right-hand side is zero in a neighborhood of the tip of the cone. The analysis of the case of a general (analytic) right-hand side will be postponed to a forthcoming paper.

In the present paper, we will introduce an ansatz on the form of the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
on the perturbed domain. Specifically, we will use a two-scale “cross-cutoff” representation:

(1.2)

u_{\varepsilon}(x)=\Phi\Big(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\Big)\,{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon](x)+\varphi(x)\,{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]\Big(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\Big)\,,

where
\varphi
and
\Phi
are cutoff functions that are
1
and
0
, respectively, near the origin and
0
and
1
, respectively, near infinity.
A similar ansatz was used by Maz’ya, Nazarov, and Plamenevskij in
[
32
, Chap. 4]
in the context of corner perturbations (very similar to the one in this paper), but for the application of asymptotic approximation methods. This approach was also employed in
[
5
]
with Caloz and Vial, and in
[
16
]
with Tordeaux and Vial. However, these papers did not address the convergence of the expansions.

We present now a more detailed outline of this novel way to generalize the FAA and of the results thus obtained.

1.3.
Storyline of the paper

We consider the Dirichlet problem for the Poisson equation in an
\varepsilon
-dependent bounded domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The family
\{\Omega_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon}
represents a “self-similar perturbation” of a limiting domain
\Omega
near its conical singularity at the origin
0
. Specifically,
\Omega
coincides with a cone
\Gamma
near
0
, and the perturbation is defined by the scaled version
\varepsilon{\mathsf{P}}
of an unbounded perturbation pattern
{\mathsf{P}}
. The three elements
\{\Omega,\Gamma,\mathcal{P}\}
(referred to as the “generating triple,” see Section
2
) completely determine
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
through the following conditions:

(i)

Outside a ball
{\mathscr{B}}
of radius
r_{0}
centered at the origin,
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
coincides with
\Omega
;

(ii)

Inside a ball
{\mathscr{B}}_{\varepsilon}
of radius
\varepsilon R_{0}
and centered at the origin,
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
coincides with the scaled pattern
\varepsilon{\mathsf{P}}
;

(iii)

In the annular transition region between the concentric balls
{\mathscr{B}}
and
{\mathscr{B}}_{\varepsilon}
, the domains
\Gamma
,
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
,
\Omega
, and
\varepsilon{\mathsf{P}}
all coincide.

Besides this, our sole geometric assumption is the
invertibility
of the Dirichlet Laplace-Beltrami operator
{\mathfrak{L}}^{\mathrm{dir}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}
on the spherical cap
\hat{\Gamma}:=\Gamma\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
of the cone
\Gamma
. In other words

the first eigenvalue
\mu_{1}
of
{\mathfrak{L}}^{\mathrm{dir}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}
is positive.

Beside this assumption, which means that the Dirichlet conditions
on
\hat{\Gamma}
do not degenerate due to a complement set of null capacity, we do not need to impose any regularity conditions on the domain
\Omega
or the cone
\Gamma
.

In Figure
1
we show an example of the rounding of a corner. The perturbation pattern
\sf P
is infinitely smooth,
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
is also smooth, but
\Omega
has a sharp corner. This scenario is well-known and notoriously hard to analyze. Due to certain technical restrictions, we had to exclude it from our paper
[
6
]
; however, it fits well into the framework of the present paper.

Figure 1.
Rounded corner: (a) Domain
\Omega
, (b)
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
(for
\varepsilon=0.2
), (c) Pattern
\sf P

r_{0}

(a)

r_{0}
\varepsilon R_{0}

(b)

R_{0}

(c)

Given a function
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})

that is zero in a neighborhood of the origin,

we consider for each
\varepsilon
the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of the Dirichlet problem

(1.3)

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{ll}u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\,,\\
\Delta u_{\varepsilon}=f\big|_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\quad\text{ in }\Omega_{\varepsilon}\,.\end{array}\end{cases}

Our goal is to describe the map
\varepsilon\mapsto u_{\varepsilon}
, for
\varepsilon
close to
0
, in terms of
convergent series expansions
in powers of
\varepsilon
.

The two scales of the problem are the natural (slow) variable
x
and the scaled (rapid) variable
X:={x}/{\varepsilon}
. For the transfer between these two scales, instead of traces, we use cutoff functions
x\mapsto\varphi(x)
(which is identically
1
for
|x|
small enough) and
X\mapsto\Phi(X)
(which is identically
1
for
|X|
large enough).

We then introduce the ansatz (
1.2
) for the form of the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
. The corresponding functions
{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]
and
{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]
are considered as independent unknowns, whereas the cutoffs
\varphi
and
\Phi
are fixed once and for all. By inserting the ansatz (
1.2
) into problem (
1.3
) we construct a block
2\times 2
operator matrix

(1.4)

{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]:=\ \begin{pmatrix}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}&{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]\\
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]&{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}\end{pmatrix}

where the diagonal terms
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}
are invertible operators in suitable function spaces on
\Omega
and
{\mathsf{P}}
, respectively, and are independent of
\varepsilon
. The anti-diagonal blocks are the transfer operators.

This matrix is directly connected to problem (
1.3
) in the sense that, if we find solutions
u={\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]
and
U={\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]
for
\varepsilon>0
to the system

(1.5)

{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]\begin{pmatrix}u\\
U\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f\big|_{\Omega}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\,,

then the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of the boundary value problem (
1.3
) is given by the cross-cutoff formula (
1.2
) (see Theorem
3.3
).

At this point, it remains to prove that
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
is invertible and to determine the structure of its inverse.

To proceed, the first ingredient is a careful analysis of the transfer operators
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]
. Using a basis of homogeneous harmonic functions related to the eigenfunctions of the Laplace-Beltrami operator
{\mathfrak{L}}^{\mathrm{dir}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}
, we derive the following expansions in operator series, along with estimates for their terms:

(1.6a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{\lambda_{j}^{+}}\mathcal{C}_{j}\quad\text{with}\quad\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|\leq A\rho^{\lambda^{+}_{j}},

(1.6b)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{-\lambda_{j}^{-}}\mathcal{B}_{j}\quad\text{with}\quad\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|\leq A\rho^{-\lambda^{-}_{j}},

for some constants
A
and
\rho>0
(cf. Theorem
4.11
).
Here the numbers
\lambda^{\pm}_{j}
are related to the eigenvalues
\mu_{j}
of
{\mathfrak{L}}^{\mathrm{dir}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}
by the formula

(1.7)

\lambda_{j}^{\pm}:=1-\frac{n}{2}\pm\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{n}{2}\right)^{2}+\mu_{j}}\,,

which is well-known in the analysis of corner problems. The
\lambda^{+}_{j}
are the primal singular exponents, and the
\lambda^{-}_{j}
are the dual ones. As we assume that the first eigenvalue
\mu_{1}
is positive, we have
\pm\lambda^{\pm}_{j}>0
for all
j
. The
set
of numbers
\pm\lambda^{\pm}_{j}
is denoted by
{\mathsf{E}}
(the exponent set). The set
{\mathsf{E}}
generates the
monoid

{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
given as

(1.8)

{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}:=\{{\mathsf{e}}={\mathsf{e}}_{1}+\cdots+{\mathsf{e}}_{k},\quad{\mathsf{e}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathsf{e}}_{k}\in{\mathsf{E}}\cup\{0\},\quad k\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}\}.

Just like
{\mathsf{E}}
, the set
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
is discrete with a smallest positive element.
In the two-dimensional example of a plane sector
\Gamma
of opening
\omega
, the set
{\mathsf{E}}
coincides with the semigroup of positive integer multiples of
\frac{\pi}{\omega}

and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}={\mathsf{E}}\cup\{0\}
.
In dimension
n\geq 3
,
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
may still coincide with
{\mathbb{N}}
as is the case when
\Gamma
is a half-space, and there are simple examples where
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
has two distinct generators, see Example
5.4
. But for the general case we have to face the situation where
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
may have an arbitrary number of generators.

Based upon the expansions (
1.6a
) and (
1.6b
), the second ingredient is the interpretation of
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
as a
generalized power series
associated with the set of exponents
{\mathsf{E}}
.
Specifically, we write

(1.9)

\begin{pmatrix}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}^{-1}&0\\
0&{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]=\mathbb{I}+\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}}\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\,\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}:=\mathbb{I}+\mathfrak{A}[\varepsilon].

As
{\mathsf{E}}
is discrete with a positive smallest element, the series
\mathbb{I}+\mathfrak{A}[\varepsilon]
is invertible in the space of
formal series
with exponent set
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}

and its inverse is given by a formal Neumann series, see Theorem
B.6
:

(1.10)

(\mathbb{I}+\mathfrak{A}[\varepsilon])^{-1}=\mathbb{I}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(-\mathfrak{A}[\varepsilon])^{k}=\mathbb{I}\ +\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}\setminus\{0\}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{e}}\,\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}.

Moreover, the estimates in (
1.6a
) and (
1.6b
), along with Weyl’s law for the eigenvalues of the Laplace-Beltrami operator, imply that
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
is a
normally convergent generalized power series

with exponent set
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
(cf. Theorem
5.2
). Namely, there exists
\varepsilon_{\star}>0
such that, in a suitable operator norm, we have

(1.11)

\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}\setminus\{0\}}\!\!\|\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{e}}\|\,\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}<\infty\quad\mbox{for $0\leq\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{\star}$.}

This implies that we can solve equation (
1.5
) for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\star})
and find expansions of
{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]
and
{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]
as convergent generalized power series with exponent set
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
:

(1.12)

{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]=\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}\,{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{e}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]=\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}\,{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{e}},

with
\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}\!\!\|{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{e}}\|\,\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}<\infty
and
\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}\!\!\|{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{e}}\|\,\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}<\infty
in energy norms.
Then we obtain a convergent multi-scale representation of the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
:

(1.13)

u_{\varepsilon}(x)=\Phi\Big(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\Big)\,\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}\,{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{e}}(x)+\varphi(x)\,\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}\,{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{e}}\Big(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\Big),\quad\forall x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}.

Though simple and efficient, this representation is not intrinsic, as it depends on the choice of the cutoff functions. Nevertheless, it allows for finding convergent inner and outer expansions for
u_{\varepsilon}
, which are intrinsic.

The traditional notion of
inner expansion
refers to an expansion in rapid variables inside a near-field region, also known as
microscopical expansion
. In our case, such an expansion takes the form of a converging linear combination of canonical profile functions:

(1.14)

u_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon X)=\sum_{j\geq 1}\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}c_{j,{\mathsf{e}}}\,\varepsilon^{{\mathsf{e}}+\lambda^{+}_{j}}K^{+}_{j}(X)\,.

Here, the
c_{j,{\mathsf{e}}}
are scalar coefficients, and the
K^{+}_{j}
are harmonic functions on the perturbation pattern
{\mathsf{P}}
, satisfying zero Dirichlet conditions and the condition at infinity:

(1.15)

K^{+}_{j}(X)\to|X|^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}\psi_{j}\left(\tfrac{X}{|X|}\right)=:h^{+}_{j}(X)\quad\text{as }|X|\to\infty\,,

where
\psi_{j}
is an eigenfunction of the Laplace-Beltrami operator
{\mathfrak{L}}^{\mathrm{dir}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}
associated with the eigenvalue
\mu_{j}
, cf. (
1.7
). The functions
h^{+}_{j}
are the singular functions of the Dirichlet problem on the limiting domain
\Omega
, as known from the seminal theory of Kondrat’ev
[
23
]
. The function
K^{+}_{j}
can be represented as the difference of
\Phi h^{+}_{j}
and a correcting function
Y^{+}_{j}
living in a variational space on
{\mathsf{P}}
:

(1.16)

K^{+}_{j}(X)=\Phi(X)h^{+}_{j}(X)-Y^{+}_{j}(X),\quad X\in{\mathsf{P}}.

We learn from a recent paper
[
22
, eq. (6)]
that such an expression can be extended to the framework of stochastic homogenization in sectors.

The sum (
1.14
) converges for
X
in
{\mathsf{P}}
inside a ball of radius
\simeq\varepsilon^{-1}
. We can rewrite it in terms of the slow variable, obtaining a sum in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
converging inside a fixed ball of radius independent of
\varepsilon
, see Theorem
6.10
.

The
outer (macroscopical) expansion
of
u_{\varepsilon}
has the form:

(1.17)

u_{\varepsilon}(x)=u_{0}(x)+\sum_{j\geq 1}\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}B_{j,{\mathsf{e}}}\,\varepsilon^{{\mathsf{e}}-\lambda^{-}_{j}}K^{-}_{j}(x)

and converges for
x
outside some ball of radius
\simeq\varepsilon
(whereas traditionally
x
is expected to lie outside some ball of radius
\simeq 1
, see Theorem
6.14
). Here,
u_{0}
is the solution of the limiting Dirichlet problem (
1.3
), the
B_{j,{\mathsf{e}}}
are scalar coefficients, and the functions
K^{-}_{j}
are non-variational solutions of the Dirichlet problem on the limiting domain
\Omega
, for which we have:

(1.18)

K^{-}_{j}(x)=\varphi(x)h^{-}_{j}(x)-Y^{-}_{j}(x),\quad x\in\Omega,

where the
h^{-}_{j}
are the dual singular functions (homogeneous of degree
\lambda^{-}_{j}
) and the
Y^{-}_{j}
are corrector functions belonging to the variational space
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
. Note that such objects are widely used for determining coefficients of singularities, see e.g.
[
34
]
,
[
15
]
.

A striking feature of the expansions (
1.14
) (written in slow variables) and (
1.17
) is their convergence in a common transitional region, which forms an annulus with radii
\simeq\varepsilon
and
\simeq 1
. This convergence in a common region allows for the identification of certain combinations of coefficients, which coincide with those utilized in the Matched Asymptotics method, as discussed in
[
16
]
. The distinctive aspect here is that these combinations emerge naturally as a byproduct of our method, whereas in the Matched Asymptotics method, they are fundamental building blocks from the outset.

1.4.
Extensions

In conclusion of this introduction, we observe that extensions of our approach are possible in several directions:

(1)

We can apply our method to domains with multiple—let’s say
L
—perturbed vertices. In such cases, instead of a
2\times 2
operator matrix forming the system
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
in (
1.1
), we would introduce a
(1+L)\times(1+L)
matrix. This matrix would include one row for the equation on the limiting domain and
L
additional rows for the equations on the perturbed vertices. Apart from this modification, the approach remains largely unchanged from the one presented here, primarily because the perturbations do not interact with one another.

(2)

We can handle mixed Dirichlet-Neumann boundary conditions, given that certain regularity assumptions are met to ensure that the Laplace-Beltrami operator with induced boundary conditions on the base of the cone
\Gamma
possesses the following properties:

(a)

It is invertible with compact resolvent;

(b)

Its spectral counting function grows at most polynomially.

(3)

We could consider a self-similar material law within the perturbed
\varepsilon
-region. Specifically, we might replace the bilinear form associated with
\nabla\cdot\nabla
with that associated with
a\left({x}/{\varepsilon}\right)\nabla\cdot\nabla
, where
a
is a bounded function such that
a\geq a_{0}
for some constant
a_{0}>0
. This formulation accounts for small inclusions or defects within the material.

By contrast, addressing pure Neumann conditions or other types of partial differential equations would require more substantial modifications of the method. The case of small holes in the interior of
\Omega
is also not directly accessible. Indeed, this scenario would correspond to setting
\Gamma={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, where our assumption on the sign of the first eigenvalue (
\mu_{1}>0
) would not hold.

Finally, if the right-hand side does not vanish identically but is
analytic inside the perturbed region, it is still possible to get
convergent expansions. This will require supplementary tools for
grouping together terms corresponding to clusters of exponents and will
be the subject of a forthcoming paper.

1.5.
Plan of the paper

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we provide the preliminaries, defining the geometric and functional setting, and the family of BVPs we will study. Section
3
introduces the Functional Analytic Approach with cutoffs and the operator matrix
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
. In Section
4
, we examine the expansion of the transfer operators
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]
. Section
5
deals with the inverse of
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
and shows that it expands to a convergent generalized power series. In Section
6
, we derive global, inner, and outer expansions of the solution of the BVP in terms of convergent generalized power series. At the end of the section, we compare the three expansions (global, inner, and outer) to derive a numerical iterative procedure that produces the coefficients. The paper concludes with two appendices. Appendix
A
addresses the relationship between a Sobolev space in the intersection of the cone with a ball and a weighted Sobolev space in the intersection of the cone with the complement of a ball. Appendix
B
introduces a simplified and adapted version of Hahn et al.’s theory of generalized power series, and also discusses the case of convergent generalized power series.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Geometric setting

Throughout the paper we use the following notation:

Notation 2.1
.

For
r>0

•

{\mathscr{B}}(r)
is the open ball of center
0
and radius
r
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

•

{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r)={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(r)}
is the complement of the closed ball
\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(r)}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

•

{\mathscr{A}}(r,r^{\prime})={\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r)\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r^{\prime})
is the open annular domain of center
0
and radii
r,r^{\prime}
.

Here are the main assumptions that the
generating triple
of open sets
\Omega
,
\Gamma
, and
{\mathsf{P}}
, involved in the definition of our self-similarly perturbed family of domains
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, must satisfy.

Assumption 2.2
.

(i)

\Gamma
is an infinite open cone in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and its section
\hat{\Gamma}
on the sphere
{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
satisfies

(2.1)

the capacity of
{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}
(as a subset of
{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
) is positive.

(ii)

\Omega
is a bounded connected open set in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that

(2.2)

\Omega\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0})=\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0})

for some
r_{0}>0
.

(iii)

{\mathsf{P}}
is an unbounded open set in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that

(2.3)

{\mathsf{P}}\cap{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(R_{0})}=\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(R_{0})}\,

for some
R_{0}>0
.

By Courtois
[
8
, Proposition 2.1]
, we can see that condition (
2.1
) is equivalent to the fact that (the space of extensions by zero of functions of)
H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma})
is a proper subspace of
H^{1}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
. Moreover, by
[
8
, Theorem 1.1 (ii)]
, we find that condition (
2.1
) is equivalent to the fact that

(2.4)

\mu_{1}>0

where
\mu_{1}
is the first eigenvalue of the positive Dirichlet Laplace-Beltrami operator
{\mathfrak{L}}^{\mathrm{dir}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}
on
\hat{\Gamma}

or, equivalently, the best constant in the Poincaré inequality

(2.5)

\mu_{1}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}\leq\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}\quad\mbox{ for all }u\in H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma})\,.

Hence property (
2.4
) is very generally satisfied:
as soon as
{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}
has positive
(n-1)
-dimensional measure or even if
{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}
is a sub-manifold of co-dimension
1
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
, (
2.1
), hence (
2.4
), holds. This implies that cracks are allowed. However, since sub-manifolds of co-dimension
\geq 2
have zero capacity, cf.
[
8
, equation (7) and Proposition 2.4 (iii)]
,
{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}
cannot be reduced to such sets (e.g. isolated points in dimension
n=3
).

We are ready for introducing the family of self-similarly perturbed domains
\{\Omega_{\varepsilon}\}
associated to the triple
\{\Omega,\Gamma,{\mathsf{P}}\}
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\{\Omega,\Gamma,{\mathsf{P}}\}
be a generating triple satisfying Assumption
2.2
.
Set

\varepsilon_{0}:=\frac{r_{0}}{R_{0}}\,,

so that
r_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}R_{0}
. Then the family
\{\Omega_{\varepsilon}\}
is defined for any
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
by the following intersection conditions

\displaystyle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0})

\displaystyle=\Omega\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0})\,,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\cap\overline{{\mathscr{A}}(\varepsilon R_{0},r_{0})}

\displaystyle=\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{A}}(\varepsilon R_{0},r_{0})}\,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\varepsilon}\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\varepsilon R_{0})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon{\mathsf{P}}\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\varepsilon R_{0})\,.

Remark 2.4
.

It is obvious that the above conditions identify
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
univocally. Moreover:

(i)
Due to condition (
2.2
),
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
coincides with
\Omega
outside
{\mathscr{B}}(\varepsilon R_{0})
. Likewise, due to (
2.3
),
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
coincides with
\varepsilon{\mathsf{P}}
inside
{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0})
.

(ii)
Hence, if we replace
r_{0}
by a smaller value
r^{\prime}_{0}
and
R_{0}
by a larger value
R^{\prime}_{0}
, then for
\varepsilon\leq\frac{r^{\prime}_{0}}{R^{\prime}_{0}}
, we obtain the same domains
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
.

(iii)
The other consequence is that, when
\varepsilon
tends to
0
, the domain
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
tends to
\Omega
as a set.

Note that, besides the conditions in (
2.1
) on the capacity of
{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}
, we do not introduce any further regularity conditions on
\Omega
and
{\mathsf{P}}
. We do not even suppose that the cone
\Gamma
or the pattern
{\mathsf{P}}
are connected. For instance, the union of a half-plane and a (disjoint) sector is an admissible
\Gamma
in dimension
2
, and the pattern
{\mathsf{P}}
may join them or not.

For an admissible example in dimension
2
, see Figure
2
, where
\Gamma
is not connected. This example also shows features that imply that neither
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
nor
\Omega
are Lipschitz: cracks and holes touching the boundary.

Figure 2.
\Omega
,
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
(for
\varepsilon=0.5
), and the perturbation pattern
{\mathsf{P}}

r_{0}

\varepsilon R_{0}
r_{0}

R_{0}

2.2.
Functional setting

We work in the standard framework of Sobolev spaces which allows for a clean variational formulation of our problems. For any open set
{\mathcal{V}}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, the space
L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})
is the space of square integrable functions on
{\mathcal{V}}
, and
H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})
is the space of
L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})
functions
g
with gradient
\nabla g
(in the distributional sense in
{\mathcal{V}}
) belonging to
L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})
. The space
H^{1}_{0}({\mathcal{V}})
is the closure in
H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})
of the space
{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{0}({\mathcal{V}})
of smooth functions with compact support in
{\mathcal{V}}
. The space
H^{-1}({\mathcal{V}})
is the dual space of
H^{1}_{0}({\mathcal{V}})
with the extension of
L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})
scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{{\mathcal{V}}}
.
We use standard notation for the norm and semi-norm in
H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})
:

\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})$}}\,}{|u|_{H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})}}{|u|_{H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})}}{|u|_{H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})}}=\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla u\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})$}}\,}{\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})}}{\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})}}{\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})}};\quad\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})$}}\,}{\|u\|_{H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})}}{\|u\|_{H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})}}{\|u\|_{H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})}}=\big(\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})$}}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})}^{2}}+\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})$}}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})}^{2}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

On any bounded domain
{\mathcal{V}}
, the semi-norm and norm of
H^{1}({\mathcal{V}})
are equivalent over
H^{1}_{0}({\mathcal{V}})
. From this, we deduce by the variational formulation and the Lax-Milgram theorem that the Laplace operator associated with the Dirichlet problem in variational form

(2.6)

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\Delta^{\sf dir}_{\mathcal{V}}:&H^{1}_{0}({\mathcal{V}})&\longrightarrow&H^{-1}({\mathcal{V}})\\
&u&\longmapsto&\Big(v\mapsto\big\langle\nabla u,\nabla v\big\rangle_{\mathcal{V}}\Big)\end{array}\quad\text{is an isomorphism.}

As an obvious particular case, we obtain that problem (
1.3
) has a unique solution in
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
for any
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
, and that the same holds for the limit problem:

Lemma 2.5
.

The Dirichlet problem on
\Omega
is uniquely solvable for any right-hand side
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
.

By contrast, the Dirichlet problem for the Laplace operator on the unbounded domain
{\mathsf{P}}
is not solvable in
H^{1}_{0}({\mathsf{P}})
, but in a larger weighted space
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})
.
For any (possibly unbounded) domain
{\mathcal{V}}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
we introduce the weighted
L^{2}
space

L^{2}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})
and its dual
L^{2}_{{\mathsf{w}}^{*}}({\mathcal{V}})
,

(2.7)

\displaystyle L^{2}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})

\displaystyle=\{G\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathcal{V}}),\quad\int_{{\mathcal{V}}}\frac{|G(X)|^{2}}{(1+|X|)^{2}}\;{\mathrm{d}}X<\infty\}

\displaystyle L^{2}_{{\mathsf{w}}^{*}}({\mathcal{V}})

\displaystyle=\{G\in L^{2}({\mathcal{V}}),\quad\int_{{\mathcal{V}}}{|G(X)|^{2}}{(1+|X|)^{2}}\;{\mathrm{d}}X<\infty\}.

Then, we define
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})
as the space with norm and semi-norm given by

(2.8)

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptG\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})$}}\,}{\|G\|_{H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})}}{\|G\|_{H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})}}{\|G\|_{H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})}}:=\left(\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptG\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})$}}^{2}}{\|G\|_{L^{2}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})}^{2}}{\|G\|_{L^{2}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})}^{2}}{\|G\|_{L^{2}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})}^{2}}+\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla G\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})$}}^{2}}{\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})}^{2}}{\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})}^{2}}{\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptG\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})$}}\,}{|G|_{H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})}}{|G|_{H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})}}{|G|_{H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})}}:=\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla G\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})$}}\,}{\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})}}{\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})}}{\|\nabla G\|_{L^{2}({\mathcal{V}})}},

and we define
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathcal{V}})
as the completion of
C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathcal{V}})
with respect to the norm of
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})
.

The following result will be proved in Section
4.1
using the Poincaré inequality (
4.8
).

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\Gamma
be a cone satisfying the capacity condition (
2.1
), and let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a domain coinciding with
\Gamma
outside a bounded set. Then the semi-norm and norm of
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}}}({\mathcal{V}})
are equivalent over
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathcal{V}})
.

By the Lax-Milgram theorem for the variational formulation of the Dirichlet problem we see that the Laplace operator induces an isomorphism from
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathcal{V}})
onto its dual. Since this dual contains the weighted space
L^{2}_{{\mathsf{w}}^{*}}({\mathcal{V}})
, as a particular case we deduce the following lemma for the pattern domain
{\mathsf{P}}
:

Lemma 2.7
.

For any
F
such that
(1+|X|)F
belongs to
L^{2}({\mathsf{P}})
, the Dirichlet problem

(2.9)

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{ll}U\in H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})\,,\\
\Delta U=F\quad\text{ in }\;{\mathsf{P}}\,,\end{array}\end{cases}

is uniquely solvable.

2.3.
Families of Dirichlet problems

With the domains
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
as in Definition
2.3
,
we consider the following family of
\varepsilon
-dependent Dirichlet problems:

(2.10)

\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{ll}u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\,,\\
\Delta u_{\varepsilon}=f_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{ in }\Omega_{\varepsilon}\,.\end{array}\end{cases}

The aim of this paper
is to find, for small values of
\varepsilon
, representations of the solutions
u_{\varepsilon}
as a convergent series in powers of
\varepsilon
, under the assumption that the right-hand sides
f_{\varepsilon}
have a uniform structure.

The first level of assumption on
f_{\varepsilon}
is

(2.11)

\exists f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\;\;\mbox{with}\;\;f\big|_{{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0})}\equiv 0\qquad\mbox{such that}\qquad f_{\varepsilon}=f\big|_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}\,.

In fact, our method of FAA with cross-cutoff is perfectly adapted to this assumption, allowing in a very natural way for a more general right-hand side. This is the second level of assumption, for which
f_{\varepsilon}
also includes some profile function
F\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
such that
F\equiv 0
on
{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(R_{0})
:

(2.12)

f_{\varepsilon}(x)=f(x)+\varepsilon^{-2}F\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right),\quad\forall x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}\,.

The third level of assumption would be to consider the case where the slow variable function
f
, instead of being
0
, is a real analytic function in the whole ball
{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0})
. This will be treated in a forthcoming paper.

3.
The Functional Analytic Approach with cross-cutoff

In our way to find representations of the solutions
u_{\varepsilon}
of problem (
2.10
), with a right-hand side satisfying (
2.11
) or, more generally, (
2.12
), we are going to translate (
2.10
) into a
2\times 2
system of equations in slow and rapid variables.

3.1.
Dedicated function spaces

Recall that
x
and
X
denote the slow and rapid variables, respectively.
To design our FAA with cross-cutoff we need to introduce some auxiliary functions, operators, and spaces. First of all we choose two smooth cutoff functions in relation with radii
r_{0}
and
R_{0}
appearing in the definition of the family of domains
\{\Omega_{\varepsilon}\}
:

•

A cutoff function
\Phi:X\mapsto\Phi(X)
that localizes in rapid variables at infinity:

(3.1)

\Phi=1\quad\mbox{on }{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(2R_{0})\,,\qquad\Phi=0\quad\mbox{on }{\mathscr{B}}(R_{0})\,,

•

A cutoff function
\varphi:x\mapsto\varphi(x)
that localizes in slow variables near
0
:

(3.2)

\varphi=1\quad\mbox{on }{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2)\,,\qquad\varphi=0\quad\mbox{on }{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0})\,.

Then we denote by
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
the change of variables

U\mapsto\left(\ x\mapsto U\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\right)

(we shall write
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U(x)
for
U(x/\varepsilon)
), so that its inverse is given by

\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}=\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}\colon u\mapsto(\ X\mapsto u(\varepsilon X)\ )

(and, accordingly, we write
\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}u(X)
for
u(\varepsilon X)
).

To deal with right-hand sides in
\Omega
that vanish in a neighborhood of the vertex and with right-hand sides in
{\mathsf{P}}
that vanish outside a ball, we define the function spaces

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}}:=\{f\in L^{2}(\Omega),\quad f\big|_{{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2)}=0\}\,,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}}:=\{F\in L^{2}({\mathsf{P}}),\quad F\big|_{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(2R_{0})}=0\}\,.

On
\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}
we consider the norms induced by
L^{2}(\Omega)
and
L^{2}({\mathsf{P}})
, respectively.
Then, we introduce two function spaces
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
and
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
for the solutions. The first one is defined by

{\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}}:=\{u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega),\quad\Delta u\in\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}\}

and we equip it with the graph norm

\|u\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}}^{2}:=\|u\|_{H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\Delta u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\,.

To define
{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}}
we use the space
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})
introduced above (Section
2.2
) and set

\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}:=\{U\in H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}}),\quad\Delta U\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}\}

with

\|U\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}}^{2}:=\|U\|_{H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})}^{2}+\|\Delta U\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\,.

Lemma 3.1
.

The Laplace operator defines isomorphisms between the above spaces, i.e.,

(3.3a)

\displaystyle\Delta_{x}\colon\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\to\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}\ \mbox{is an isomorphism}\,,

(3.3b)

\displaystyle\Delta_{X}\colon\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}\ \mbox{is an isomorphism}\,.

Proof.

As the space
\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}
embeds canonically in
H^{-1}(\Omega)
, (
3.3a
) is an obvious consequence of Lemma
2.5
. Concerning (
3.3b
), the argument relies on Lemma
2.7
if we notice that the space
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}
embeds in the dual of the variational space
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})
(even if now the norm of the embedding depends on
R_{0}
).
∎

3.2.
The operator matrix
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]

The equivalence between the Dirichlet problem (
2.10
) and a
2\times 2
system of equations on
\Omega
or
{\mathsf{P}}
will be proved for right-hand sides
f_{\varepsilon}
satisfying the second level of assumption (
2.12
). So, let
f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}
and
F\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}
be given. Both functions can be extended by
0
, thus defining elements of
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
and giving sense to the equality
f_{\varepsilon}=f+\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}F
in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
. The idea underlying the construction of the
2\times 2
system is to consider a similar ansatz for the solution and the right-hand sides.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}/4]
. We note that, due to the specific supports of
\Phi
and
\varphi
, we have the equalities
f=(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi)\,f
and
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}F=\varphi\,(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}F)
. Hence, we find it convenient to write our family of Dirichlet problems as

(3.4)

\begin{cases}u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})\,,\\
\Delta u_{\varepsilon}=(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi)\,f+\varepsilon^{-2}\varphi\,\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}F\ \mbox{ in }\ \Omega_{\varepsilon}\,.\end{cases}

Then, for any
fixed

\varepsilon
,
we look for a representation of the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of (
3.4
) in a form that resembles the right-hand side. Specifically, we write

(3.5)

u_{\varepsilon}=(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi)\,u+\varphi\,\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U

(cf. (
1.2
)), where the functions
u:x\mapsto u(x)
and
U:X\mapsto U(X)
belong to
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
and
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
, respectively. These functions
u
and
U
depend on
\varepsilon
and will be eventually described in terms of convergent generalized power series of
\varepsilon
denoted by
{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]
and
{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]
. For the moment being, we do not need to track the dependence in
\varepsilon
and just write
u
and
U
.

The next theorem relies on the calculation of the Laplace operator acting on
(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi)\,u+\varphi\,\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U
. Hence the commutator of
\Delta
with cutoffs appears naturally:

Notation 3.2
.

For a smooth function
\psi
, let the commutator
[\Delta,\psi]
be defined as

[\Delta,\psi]w=\Delta(\psi w)-\psi\Delta w=2\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla w+(\Delta\psi)w.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}/4]
be chosen.
Let
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
be defined as the
2\times 2
block operator matrix

(3.6)

{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]:=\ \begin{pmatrix}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}&{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]\\
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]&{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}\end{pmatrix}

from
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
to
\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}
, where the entries
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}
, and
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}
are defined by

(3.7)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}\ \colon

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\to\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}\,,

\displaystyle u\longmapsto\Delta_{x}u

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}\ \colon

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}\,,

\displaystyle U\longmapsto\Delta_{X}U

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]\ \colon

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}\,,

\displaystyle U\longmapsto[\Delta_{x},\varphi](\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]\ \colon

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\to\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}\,,

\displaystyle u\longmapsto[\Delta_{X},\Phi](\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}u)\,.

Let
f
belong to
\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}
and
F
to
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}
.
If we have

(3.8)

{{\mathcal{M}}}[\varepsilon]\left(\begin{array}[]{l}u\\
U\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}f\\
F\end{array}\right)

for a pair of functions
(u,U)\in\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
, then the function

w_{\varepsilon}:=(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi)u+\varphi(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U)

is a solution of the boundary value problem (
3.4
) and, therefore, coincides with the unique solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of
(
3.4
)
.

Proof.

We have first to check that the constitutive operators of
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
are well defined. Concerning the diagonal entries, this is obvious. Concerning non-diagonal entries, this is an easy consequence of the following facts

•

The supports of
x\mapsto\nabla_{x}\varphi
and
x\mapsto\Delta_{x}\varphi
are contained in the annulus
{\mathscr{A}}(\frac{r_{0}}{2},r_{0})
, and the supports of
X\mapsto\nabla_{X}\Phi
and
X\mapsto\Delta_{X}\Phi
are contained in
{\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0})
,

•

The operators
\nabla_{x}
and
\nabla_{X}
are bounded from
H^{1}
to
L^{2}
.

Then, we have

(3.9)

\Delta w_{\varepsilon}=g_{\varepsilon,1}+g_{\varepsilon,2}\quad\mbox{with}\quad g_{\varepsilon,1}=\Delta_{x}\{(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi)\,u\}\quad\mbox{and}\quad g_{\varepsilon,2}=\Delta_{x}\{\varphi(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U)\}\,,

and we compute

\displaystyle g_{\varepsilon,1}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi)\Delta_{x}u+2\nabla_{x}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi)\cdot\nabla_{x}u+(\Delta_{x}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi))u

\displaystyle g_{\varepsilon,2}

\displaystyle=\varphi\Delta_{x}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U)+2\nabla_{x}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{x}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U)+({\Delta_{x}\varphi})\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U\,.

Since
u
is in
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
, by definition
\Delta_{x}u
belongs to
\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}
, so it is
\equiv 0
on
{\mathscr{B}}(\frac{r_{0}}{2})
. As
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi
is
\equiv 1
on
{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(2\varepsilon R_{0})
and
2\varepsilon R_{0}<\frac{r_{0}}{2}
, we find that the first term of
g_{\varepsilon,1}
satisfies

(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi)\Delta_{x}u=\Delta_{x}u.

Concerning the first term of
g_{\varepsilon,2}
, we start with the identity

\varphi\Delta_{x}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U)=\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\big\{(\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}\varphi)\Delta_{X}U\big\}

and, since
U
is in
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
, we can deduce by a similar reasoning as above that
(\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}\varphi)
is
\equiv 1
on the support of
\Delta_{X}U
, ending up with

\varphi\Delta_{x}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U)=\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\big\{\Delta_{X}U\big\}\,,

hence

g_{\varepsilon,2}=\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\big\{\Delta_{X}U\big\}+2\nabla_{x}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{x}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U)+({\Delta_{x}\varphi})\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U\,.

Finally, noting that

2\nabla_{x}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi)\cdot\nabla_{x}u+(\Delta_{x}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Phi))u=\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Big\{2\nabla_{X}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{X}(\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}u)+(\Delta_{X}\Phi)\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}u\Big\}

we obtain

g_{\varepsilon,1}=\Delta_{x}u+\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\Big\{2\nabla_{X}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{X}(\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}u)+(\Delta_{X}\Phi)\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}u\Big\}\,.

Now, developing (
3.8
) we find

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\Delta_{x}u+2\nabla_{x}\varphi\cdot\nabla_{x}(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U)+(\Delta_{x}\varphi)\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U\,,

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=\Delta_{X}U+2\nabla_{X}\Phi\cdot\nabla_{X}(\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}u)+(\Delta_{X}\Phi)\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}u\,.

So
g_{\varepsilon,1}+g_{\varepsilon,2}
coincides with
f+\varepsilon^{-2}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}F
, which in view of (
3.9
), concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

1) From the proof above, the rationale for the construction of
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
appears more clearly. The diagonal terms have a simple structure thanks to the conditions on the supports of
\Phi
and
\varphi
, whereas the non-diagonal terms can be seen as transfer operators between slow and rapid variables: Here the cutoff dictates the choice of variables (slow when
\varphi
is present, rapid when
\Phi
is present).

2) The solvability of equation (
3.8
) will be proven later on showing that
{{\mathcal{M}}}[\varepsilon]
is invertible for
\varepsilon\geq 0
small enough (cf. Theorem
5.2
).

4.
The spectral expansion of transfer operators

The FAA strategy boils down to understanding in which sense the matrix
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
can be seen as an “analytic” function of
\varepsilon
as
\varepsilon
tends to
0
. In this section we show how this is related to the spectral expansion at
0
or at infinity of harmonic functions satisfying Dirichlet conditions in the cone
\Gamma
. Consequently we will have to extend the notion of analytic structure to generalized convergent series with non-integer exponents in
\varepsilon
.

4.1.
Energy spaces on cones

We first have to collect some basic results about Sobolev spaces and the Laplace operator in our conical domains, which, due to the very weak regularity assumptions, cannot just be quoted from the standard literature on domains with conical singularities
[
23
,
25
,
26
]
. Recall that the spherical domain

\hat{\Gamma}=\Gamma\cap{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
is an arbitrary open subset of the unit sphere that satisfies the single condition that the capacity of its complement is non-zero. Otherwise, it can have cracks, cusps, infinitely many holes or connected components, and so on. Its boundary can be very wild. Therefore the truncated cone

\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)
has a boundary with two very different parts:
The lateral boundary
(\partial\Gamma)\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)}
, where we want to impose Dirichlet conditions, does not satisfy any of the usual conditions required for defining boundary traces in Sobolev spaces, but the remaining part is the spherical cap
\rho\hat{\Gamma}
that is part of a smooth manifold, and the boundary traces on this part are well defined and will play an important role.

We begin by introducing some notation for spaces where the zero boundary condition is imposed only on parts of the boundary.

Notation 4.1
.

Let
\rho>0
. We define

(i)

C^{\infty}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
as the space of restrictions to
\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)}
of smooth functions with support in
\Gamma
, and define similarly
C^{\infty}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
,

(ii)

H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
as the closure of
C^{\infty}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
in
H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))
,

(iii)

H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
as the closure of
C^{\infty}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
in
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}}}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho))
.

For
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
, we use polar coordinates
r=|x|
and

\vartheta=\hat{x}=\frac{x}{|x|}
, where we write indifferently
\hat{x}
for a unit vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
\vartheta
for the same considered as a point of the manifold
{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
. The gradient
\nabla=\nabla_{x}
splits into a radial and tangential part
\nabla=\hat{x}\partial_{r}+\nabla_{T}
, which by the orthogonality relation
\hat{x}\cdot\nabla_{T}=0
, gives the formulas

(4.1)

\nabla u=\partial_{r}u\,\hat{x}+\nabla_{T}u\,;\quad\partial_{r}u=\hat{x}\cdot\nabla u\,;\quad\nabla_{T}u=\nabla u-(\hat{x}\cdot\nabla u)\hat{x}\,.

If we write
\tilde{u}(r,\vartheta)=u(x)
(the tilde may be omitted later on), we have

\nabla_{T}u(x)=\frac{1}{r}\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\tilde{u}(r,\vartheta)\,,\quad\mbox{where $\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}$ is the tangential gradient on the unit sphere}.

Pythagoras’ theorem implies that

|\nabla u|^{2}=|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}u|^{2}\,,

and integrating over an annular domain
{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2})
results in

(4.2)

\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}))$}}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}))}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}))}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}))}^{2}}=\int_{{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2})}\!|\nabla u|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}\!\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\Big(|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}|\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}u|^{2}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\,r^{n-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}r

for any
u\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Gamma)
.
We can choose
r_{1}=0
and get for any
\rho>0

(4.3)

\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))$}}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}^{2}}=\int_{0}^{\rho}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\Big(r^{n-1}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+r^{n-3}|\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}u|^{2}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\,{\mathrm{d}}r\,,

which is, by density, valid for all
u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
.
Likewise, we can choose
r_{2}=\infty
and get

(4.4)

\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho))$}}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho))}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho))}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho))}^{2}}=\int_{\rho}^{\infty}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\Big(r^{n-1}|\partial_{r}u|^{2}+r^{n-3}|\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}u|^{2}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\,{\mathrm{d}}r

for all

u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
.

Now we can employ the Poincaré inequality (
2.5
) to analyze the spherical traces on
|x|=r
.

Lemma 4.2
.

(i) For any
r>0
and

v\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Gamma)
define
\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}v
as the function defined on
\hat{\Gamma}
by

\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}v(\hat{x})=v(r\hat{x}),\quad\hat{x}\in\hat{\Gamma}.

Let
\rho>0
. Then for
r\in(0,\rho]
, the mapping
\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}
has an extension to a bounded operator

\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}:H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})\to L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})

satisfying an estimate

(4.5)

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}\,}{\|\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}}{\|\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}}{\|\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}}\leq C_{\hat{\Gamma}}\,r^{1-\frac{n}{2}}\,\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))$}}\,}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}}\,.

Similarly, for
r\in[\rho,\infty)
, the mapping
\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}
has an extension to a bounded operator

\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}:H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})\to L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})

satisfying an estimate

(4.6)

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}\,}{\|\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}}{\|\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}}{\|\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}}\leq C_{\hat{\Gamma}}\,r^{1-\frac{n}{2}}\,\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho))$}}\,}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho))}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho))}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho))}}\,.

Here the constant
C_{\hat{\Gamma}}
depends only on the dimension
n
and the first Dirichlet eigenvalue
\mu_{1}
of the set
\hat{\Gamma}
, cf (
2.4
).

(ii) Let
u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
.
Then the function
r\mapsto\tilde{u}(r)=\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u
belongs to the vector valued weighted
H^{1}
-Sobolev space
\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))
with norm given by

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}:=\int_{0}^{\rho}\big(\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}+\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}\big)\,r^{n-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}r\,.

For almost every
r\in(0,\rho]
,
\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u
is in
H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma})
, and
\tilde{u}
belongs to the vector-valued weighted
L^{2}
space

\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))
with norm given by

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}:=\int_{0}^{\rho}\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{|\tilde{u}(r)|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{|\tilde{u}(r)|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{|\tilde{u}(r)|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}\,r^{n-3}\,{\mathrm{d}}r.

Likewise, for the unbounded truncated cone
\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)
and

u\in H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
, the analogous norms

\int_{\rho}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\frac{\tilde{u}(r)}{1+r}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|\frac{\tilde{u}(r)}{1+r}\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\frac{\tilde{u}(r)}{1+r}\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\frac{\tilde{u}(r)}{1+r}\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}\,r^{n-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}r\,,\;\int_{\rho}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}\,r^{n-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}r\;\mbox{ and }\;\int_{\rho}^{\infty}\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{|\tilde{u}(r)|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{|\tilde{u}(r)|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{|\tilde{u}(r)|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}\,r^{n-3}\,{\mathrm{d}}r

are finite.

Proof.

(i)
We first show that the bound (
4.5
) holds for
u\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Gamma)
and for
r=\rho
(from which it follows for
r\leq\rho)
: We have with
\tilde{u}(r)=\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u

\displaystyle\rho^{n-2}|\tilde{u}(\rho)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\rho}\partial_{r}\big(r^{n-2}|\tilde{u}(r)|^{2}\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}r

\displaystyle=(n-2)\int_{0}^{\rho}r^{n-3}|\tilde{u}(r)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}r+2\int_{0}^{\rho}r^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\tilde{u}(r)\,r^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\,{\mathrm{d}}r\,.

With the Young inequality, this gives for any
\eta>0

\rho^{n-2}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}(\rho)\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(\rho)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(\rho)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(\rho)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}\leq(n-2+\eta)\int_{0}^{\rho}r^{n-3}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}\,{\mathrm{d}}y+\tfrac{1}{\eta}\int_{0}^{\rho}r^{n-1}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}\,{\mathrm{d}}r\,.

At this point, we use the Poincaré inequality (
2.5
)

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\mu_{1}}\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{|\tilde{u}(r)|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{|\tilde{u}(r)|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{|\tilde{u}(r)|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}

and compare with (
4.3
) to obtain (choosing
\eta=1
)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}(\rho)\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(\rho)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(\rho)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(\rho)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\rho^{2-n}\int_{0}^{\rho}\big\{r^{n-1}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}+\tfrac{n-1}{\mu_{1}}\,r^{n-3}\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{|\tilde{u}(r)|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{|\tilde{u}(r)|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{|\tilde{u}(r)|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}\big\}\,{\mathrm{d}}r

\displaystyle\leq C_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{2}\,\rho^{2-n}\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))$}}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}^{2}}\;\mbox{ with }\;C_{\hat{\Gamma}}=\max\{1,\sqrt{\tfrac{n-1}{\mu_{1}}}\}\,.

Then, the estimate in (
4.5
) extends by continuity to all of
H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
. The analogous estimate for the unbounded truncated cone is obtained by integrating from
\rho
to
\infty
.

(ii)
The proof is then easily completed: The identity (
4.3
) for the
H^{1}
seminorm can be written for the
H^{1}
norm with the above definitions as

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))$}}^{2}}{\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}^{2}}{\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}^{2}}{\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\rho}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\Big(r^{n-1}|\tilde{u}|^{2}+r^{n-1}|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}|^{2}+r^{n-3}|\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\tilde{u}|^{2}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\,{\mathrm{d}}r

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}+\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}\,.

The fact that
\tilde{u}(r)\in H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma})
for almost every
r\in(0,\rho]
follows from Fubini’s theorem for the latter integral.
∎

We can now prove the Poincaré inequalities that imply the proof of Lemma
2.6
. We choose as a domain the truncated cone
\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2})
, where

0\leq r_{1}<r_{2}\leq+\infty
. The truncated cones
\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{2})
and

\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{1})
are the special cases
r_{1}=0
and
r_{2}=+\infty
, respectively.
From the Poincaré inequality on
\hat{\Gamma}
, we obtain by integrating with the weight
r^{n-3}
:

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tfrac{u}{r}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}))$}}^{2}}{\|\tfrac{u}{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}))}^{2}}{\|\tfrac{u}{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}))}^{2}}{\|\tfrac{u}{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}))}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}r^{n-3}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}r\leq\frac{1}{\mu_{1}}\int_{r_{1}}^{r_{2}}r^{n-3}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\tilde{u}(r)\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\tilde{u}(r)\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}r

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\mu_{1}}\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}))$}}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}))}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}))}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{1},r_{2}))}^{2}}

(see also (
4.2
)).
In the bounded case
r_{2}<\infty
, we can use the trivial estimate

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{2}))$}}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{2}))}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{2}))}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{2}))}^{2}}\leq r_{2}^{2}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tfrac{u}{r}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{2}))$}}^{2}}{\|\tfrac{u}{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{2}))}^{2}}{\|\tfrac{u}{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{2}))}^{2}}{\|\tfrac{u}{r}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{2}))}^{2}}

to get rid of the weight in the
L^{2}
norm, but in the unbounded case for
\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{1})
we need to keep it. In the latter case, the weight
\frac{1}{r}
is equivalent to the weight
\frac{1}{1+r}
, which is the one used in Lemma
2.6
.

4.2.
Laplace-Beltrami eigenfunction expansion

The bilinear form
\big\langle\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}u,\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}v\big\rangle_{\hat{\Gamma}}
is, on one hand, the scalar product in the Hilbert space
H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma})
encountered in the previous section. On the other hand, it is the energy form associated with the Laplace-Beltrami operator on the sphere
{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
:

\big\langle\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}u,\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}v\big\rangle_{\hat{\Gamma}}=\big\langle{\mathfrak{L}}^{{\mathrm{dir}}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}u,v\big\rangle_{\hat{\Gamma}}.

This relation defines the variational form of the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
\hat{\Gamma}
with Dirichlet conditions as a bounded operator

{\mathfrak{L}}^{\mathrm{dir}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}:H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma})\longrightarrow H^{-1}(\hat{\Gamma})\,.

It can also be extended to an unbounded self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
with domain

\operatorname{\rm Dom}({\mathfrak{L}}^{\mathrm{dir}}_{\hat{\Gamma}})=\{\hat{u}\in H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}),\ \Delta_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\hat{u}\in L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})\}\,.

Due to the compact embedding of
H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma})
in
L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
, which is valid for any open subset
\hat{\Gamma}
of
{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
, it has a compact resolvent and therefore a discrete spectrum, and the eigenvalues can be obtained from the min-max principle applied to the quadratic energy form.

The main tool for the analysis of elements in our special spaces
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
and
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
is the projection on an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions of
{\mathfrak{L}}^{\mathrm{dir}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}
.
Let us therefore recall some well-known facts about such eigenfunction expansions and then apply them to our special situation of conical singularities.

The role of the assumption (
2.1
) is to ensure that
{\mathfrak{L}}^{{\mathrm{dir}}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}
is positive definite, that is, its lowest eigenvalue
\mu_{1}
is non-zero.

Notation 4.3
.

Let

0<\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}\leq\ldots\mu_{j}\leq\ldots

be the non-decreasing sequence of the eigenvalues of
