{\mathfrak{L}}^{\mathrm{dir}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}
(with possible repetitions according to multiplicities) and let
\{\psi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
be an associated basis of eigenfunctions orthonormal in
L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
,
i.e.

\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\psi_{i}\psi_{j}\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma=\delta_{i,j}
for all
i,j\geq 1
.

The eigenfunctions
\psi_{j}
satisfy, by definition

\forall v\in H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}),\quad\big\langle\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\psi_{j},\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}v\big\rangle_{\hat{\Gamma}}=\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\mu_{j}\psi_{j}\,v\;{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\,.

In particular, they are orthogonal in
H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma})
:

\forall k\geq 1,\quad\big\langle\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\psi_{j},\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\psi_{k}\big\rangle_{\hat{\Gamma}}=\delta_{j,k}\,\mu_{j}\,.

Any
u\in L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
has a convergent expansion

(4.9)

u=\sum_{k\geq 1}u_{j}\psi_{j}\quad\mbox{ with coefficients }u_{j}=\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}u\,\psi_{j}\;{\mathrm{d}}\sigma

with equality of norms

(4.10)

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}=\sum_{j\geq 1}|u_{j}|^{2}\,.

It is also well known that
u
belongs to
H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma})
if and only if

\sum_{j\geq 1}\mu_{j}|u_{j}|^{2}<\infty
, and then the expansion (
4.9
) converges in

H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma})
with equality of norms

(4.11)

\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})$}}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}^{2}}=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mu_{j}|u_{j}|^{2}\,.

We now apply this to the description of
H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
in polar coordinates obtained in the previous section, in particular the expression (
4.7
) for the norm. Let
u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
and define the coefficient functions

r\mapsto\tilde{u}_{j}(r)

(4.12)

\tilde{u}_{j}(r)=\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u(\vartheta)\,\psi_{j}(\vartheta)\;{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{\vartheta}\ =\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\tilde{u}(r)\,\psi_{j}\;{\mathrm{d}}\sigma\,.

From Lemma
4.2
, we immediately deduce that these coefficients belong to the scalar versions
\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho):=\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);\mathbb{R})
and
\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho):=\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);\mathbb{R})
of the weighted
L^{2}
and
H^{1}
spaces defined above. Moreover, we have the norm equalities

(4.13)

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mu_{j}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)}^{2}}\!\quad\mbox{and}\quad\!\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))}^{2}}=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)}^{2}}\,.

Hence, the following result:

Proposition 4.4
.

For
\rho>0
and
u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
, let the coefficients
\tilde{u}_{j}(r)
be defined by (
4.12
). Then the expansion

(4.14)

u(x)=\sum_{j\geq 1}\tilde{u}_{j}(r)\psi_{j}(\vartheta),\quad x=r\vartheta\in\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)}

converges in
H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
and

(4.15)

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})$}}^{2}}{\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})}^{2}}{\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})}^{2}}{\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})}^{2}}=\sum_{j\geq 1}\Big(\mu_{j}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)}^{2}}+\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)}^{2}}\Big)\,.

Here

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)}^{2}}=\int_{0}^{\rho}|\tilde{u}_{j}(r)|^{2}\,r^{n-3}\,{\mathrm{d}}r\>\>\mbox{ and }\>\>\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\tilde{u}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)$}}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|\tilde{u}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)}^{2}}=\int_{0}^{\rho}\big(|\tilde{u}_{j}(r)|^{2}+|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}_{j}(r)|^{2}\big)\,r^{n-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}r\,.

Similarly, for
u\in H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
the expansion
(
4.14
) converges in
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
, and

(4.16)

\mathchoice{|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})$}}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})}^{2}}{|u|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})}^{2}}=\sum_{j\geq 1}\Big(\mu_{j}\int_{\rho}^{\infty}|\tilde{u}_{j}(r)|^{2}\,r^{n-3}\,{\mathrm{d}}r+\int_{\rho}^{\infty}|\partial_{r}\tilde{u}_{j}(r)|^{2}\,r^{n-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}r\Big)\,.

In the proof leading to this proposition, the only thing that has not yet been mentioned is that the terms
\tilde{u}_{j}(r)\psi_{j}(\vartheta)

in the expansion (
4.14
) actually belong to the space

H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
. This is an easy consequence of the following observation, because for the approximation of
\tilde{u}_{j}(r)\psi_{j}(\vartheta)
by
C_{0}^{\infty}
functions, one can approximate both factors separately by
C_{0}^{\infty}
functions.

Lemma 4.5
.

The subspace
C_{0}^{\infty}((0,\rho])
is dense in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)\cap\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)
.

Proof.

The norm in
\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)\cap\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)
is equivalent to the Kondrat’ev-type homogeneous weighted
H^{1}
norm
\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptv\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle K^{1}_{\alpha}(0,\rho)$}}\,}{\|v\|_{K^{1}_{\alpha}(0,\rho)}}{\|v\|_{K^{1}_{\alpha}(0,\rho)}}{\|v\|_{K^{1}_{\alpha}(0,\rho)}}
with

\alpha=\frac{n-3}{2}
defined by

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptv\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle K^{1}_{\alpha}(0,\rho)$}}^{2}}{\|v\|_{K^{1}_{\alpha}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|v\|_{K^{1}_{\alpha}(0,\rho)}^{2}}{\|v\|_{K^{1}_{\alpha}(0,\rho)}^{2}}=\int_{0}^{\rho}\Big(r^{2\alpha}|v(r)|^{2}+r^{2\alpha+2}|v^{\prime}(r)|^{2}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}r\,.

A short way to prove that
C_{0}^{\infty}((0,\rho])
is dense in

K^{1}_{\alpha}(0,\rho)
is the following:

Define
\hat{v}(t)=e^{-(\alpha+\frac{1}{2})t}v(\rho e^{-t})
. Then it is easy to verify that the mapping
v\mapsto\hat{v}
is an isomorphism from
K^{1}_{\alpha}(0,\rho)
onto the (unweighted) Sobolev space

H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{+})
, and
C_{0}^{\infty}(\overline{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}})
is well known to be dense in
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}_{+})
.
∎

The expansion presented in Proposition
4.4
can be used to further elucidate the structure of our energy space
H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))}
. We mention two results that are valid under our general weak regularity assumptions.

Corollary 4.6
.

For any
\rho>0
there holds

H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})=\mathcal{L}^{2}((0,\rho);H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma}))\cap\mathcal{H}^{1}((0,\rho);L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}))\,.

Proof.

Lemma
4.2
and the equality of norms (
4.7
) imply that the left-hand space is contained as a closed subspace in the right-hand space. The reverse inclusion follows from the expansion (
4.14
) in Proposition
4.4
, because this expansion converges in the same way for all elements of the right-hand space.
∎

Corollary 4.7
.

Another consequence is the implication

u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})\quad\&\quad\operatorname{\rm tr}_{\rho}u=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))\,.

Proof.

Indeed, if
\operatorname{\rm tr}_{\rho}u=0
, then for all
j
,
\tilde{u}_{j}(\rho)=0
. This implies that
\tilde{u}_{j}
can be approximated in the weighted
K^{1}_{\alpha}
norm by
C_{0}^{\infty}
functions with support in
(0,\rho)
, and therefore every term in the expansion (
4.14
) is in
H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))
.
∎

Analogous results hold on the unbounded truncated cone for
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
. They can be obtained without effort by using the Kelvin transformation, see Appendix
A
.

4.3.
Expansion of harmonic functions

Let us assume that
u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
is harmonic in

\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)
. We will determine the form of the coefficients
\tilde{u}_{j}
in the expansion (
4.14
) by the method of separation of variables in polar coordinates. The function
u
satisfies
\Delta u=0
in the distributional sense, so that

\forall v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)}),\quad\big\langle\nabla u,\nabla v\big\rangle_{\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)}}=0.

Choose
j
. Taking
v(x)=\xi(r)\psi_{j}(\vartheta)
, we find that, for any
\xi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(0,\rho)
, we have

\int_{0}^{\rho}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\Big(\partial_{r}\tilde{u}(r,\vartheta)\,\xi^{\prime}(r)\psi_{j}(\vartheta)+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\tilde{u}(r,\vartheta)\cdot\xi(r)\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\psi_{j}\Big){\mathrm{d}}\vartheta\;r^{n-1}{\mathrm{d}}r=0\,.

Combined with

\Big\langle\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\tilde{u}(r,\cdot),\nabla_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}\psi_{j}\Big\rangle_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}=\mu_{j}\tilde{u}_{j}
,
this gives the variational form

\int_{0}^{\rho}\Big(\tilde{u}^{\prime}_{j}(r)\,\xi^{\prime}(r)+\frac{\mu_{j}}{r^{2}}\,\tilde{u}_{j}(r)\,\xi(r)\Big)\;r^{n-1}{\mathrm{d}}r=0

of the differential equation

(4.17)

-r^{1-n}\big(r^{n-1}\tilde{u}^{\prime}_{j}(r)\big)^{\prime}+\frac{\mu_{j}}{r^{2}}\,\tilde{u}_{j}(r)=0\,.

The solutions have the form
r^{\lambda}
with
\lambda
such that

(4.18)

-\lambda(\lambda+n-2)+\mu_{j}=0.

Hence, there exist coefficients
c^{+}_{j}
and
c^{-}_{j}
such that

(4.19)

\tilde{u}_{j}(r)=c^{+}_{j}r^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}+c^{-}_{j}r^{\lambda^{-}_{j}}

with

(4.20)

\lambda_{j}^{\pm}:=1-\frac{n}{2}\pm\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{n}{2}\right)^{2}+\mu_{j}}\,,

the two roots of equation (
4.18
).

We note that
\{\lambda^{+}_{j}\}
is a non-decreasing sequence of positive numbers and
\lambda^{-}_{j}
satisfies

(4.21)

\lambda_{j}^{-}=2-n-\lambda_{j}^{+}.

One readily checks that for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, the function
r\mapsto r^{\lambda}
belongs to our weighted space
\mathcal{L}^{2}(0,\rho)\cap\mathcal{H}^{1}(0,\rho)
if and only if
\lambda>1-\frac{n}{2}
. Therefore, we have
c^{-}_{j}=0
in (
4.19
), and we conclude that

\tilde{u}_{j}(r)=c^{+}_{j}r^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}.

Taking

(4.22)

h^{+}_{j}(x):=r^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}\psi_{j}(\vartheta),

we can write the expansion (
4.14
) as

u=\sum_{j\geq 1}c^{+}_{j}h^{+}_{j},

where the sum converges in
H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho))
. We also note that for the coefficients
c^{+}_{j}
, we have the equality

(4.23)

c^{+}_{j}=r^{-\lambda^{+}_{j}}\tilde{u}_{j}(r)=r^{-\lambda^{+}_{j}}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}u(\vartheta)\,\psi_{j}(\vartheta)\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{\vartheta}.

Similarly, for a function
U\in H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
, we can find the expansion

(4.24)

U=\sum_{j\geq 1}B^{-}_{j}h^{-}_{j},

with

(4.25)

h^{-}_{j}(x):=r^{\lambda^{-}_{j}}\psi_{j}(\vartheta),

and

(4.26)

B^{-}_{j}=r^{-\lambda^{-}_{j}}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\operatorname{\rm tr}_{r}U(\vartheta)\,\psi_{j}(\vartheta)\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{\vartheta}.

The series in (
4.24
) converges in
H^{1}_{\text{w}}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho))
, and the expansion can be proved following the same steps as in the proof for the bounded domain. In particular, we recover the same differential equation (
4.17
), leading to the same solution (
4.19
). However, this time we must set the coefficient of
r^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}
to zero, because
r^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}
does not belong to the corresponding space
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\rho,+\infty)\cap\mathcal{H}^{1}(\rho,+\infty)
. Alternatively, a proof can be obtained using the Kelvin transform isomorphism between
H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
and
H^{1}_{\text{w},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
, see Appendix
A
.

For the functions
h^{+}_{j}
and
h^{-}_{j}
, we have the following:

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\rho>0
. The homogeneous harmonic functions
h^{\pm}_{j}
of (
4.22
) and (
4.25
) have the following properties:

a) The functions
h^{+}_{j}
belong to
H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
and satisfy the orthogonality relations

(4.27a)

\int_{\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)}\nabla h^{+}_{i}\cdot\nabla h^{+}_{j}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=\lambda^{+}_{j}\rho^{2\lambda^{+}_{j}+n-2}\,\delta_{i,j}\,.

b) The functions
h^{-}_{j}
belong to
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
and satisfy the orthogonality relations

(4.27b)

\int_{\Gamma\cap{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)}}\nabla h^{-}_{i}\cdot\nabla h^{-}_{j}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=-\lambda^{-}_{j}\rho^{2\lambda^{-}_{j}+n-2}\,\delta_{i,j}\,.

Proof.

The orthogonality of the families
\{h^{\pm}_{j}\}_{j}
follows from the orthogonality of
\{\psi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
in both
L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma})
and
H^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})
. It remains to compute the
H^{1}
seminorms, using (
4.3
). We find with

(\lambda^{+}_{j})^{2}+\mu_{j}=\lambda^{+}_{j}(2\lambda^{+}_{j}+n-2)

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)}|\nabla h^{+}_{i}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\rho}\big(|\lambda^{+}_{j}r^{\lambda^{+}_{j}-1}|^{2}r^{n-1}+|r^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}|^{2}r^{n-3}\mu_{j}\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}r

\displaystyle=\big((\lambda^{+}_{j})^{2}+\mu_{j}\big)\int_{0}^{\rho}r^{2\lambda^{+}_{j}+n-3}\,{\mathrm{d}}r=\lambda^{+}_{j}\rho^{2\lambda^{+}_{j}+n-2}\,.

The proof for (
4.27b
) is similar.
∎

We can now summarize the result about the expansion in the basis of the harmonic extensions
h^{\pm}_{j}
of the Laplace-Beltrami eigenfunctions
\psi_{j}
. The proof of Theorem
4.9
follows from Proposition
4.4
, with the special form (
4.23
) and (
4.26
) of the coefficient functions.

Theorem 4.9
.

Choose
\rho>0
and recall that
\operatorname{\rm tr}_{\rho}
is the trace operator defined in Lemma
4.2
.

a)
For all
j\geq 1
, we denote by
\mathbf{c}_{j}
the functional

(4.28)

\mathbf{c}_{j}\colon

H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})

\to

{\mathbb{R}}

u

\mapsto

\displaystyle\rho^{-\lambda^{+}_{j}}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\operatorname{\rm tr}_{\rho}u(\vartheta)\;\psi_{j}(\vartheta)\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{\vartheta}\,.

If
u\in H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
is harmonic,
then the sequence
\{\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\}
satisfies:

(4.29)

\sum_{j\geq 1}\lambda^{+}_{j}\rho^{2\lambda^{+}_{j}+n-2}\,|\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)|^{2}=\int_{\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)}|\nabla u|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x

and we have the representation formula for
u
:

(4.30)

u=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,h^{+}_{j}\quad\mbox{with convergence in $H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})$.}

b)
For all
j\geq 1
, we denote by
\mathbf{B}_{j}
the functional

(4.31)

\mathbf{B}_{j}\colon

H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})

\to

{\mathbb{R}}

U

\mapsto

\displaystyle\rho^{-\lambda^{-}_{j}}\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\operatorname{\rm tr}_{\rho}U(\vartheta)\;\psi_{j}(\vartheta)\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{\vartheta}\,.

If
U\in H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
is harmonic,
then the sequence
\{\mathbf{B}_{j}(U)\}
satisfies:

(4.32)

-\sum_{j\geq 1}\lambda^{-}_{j}\rho^{2\lambda^{-}_{j}+n-2}\,|\mathbf{B}_{j}(U)|^{2}=\int_{\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)}|\nabla U|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}X

and we have the representation formula for
U
:

(4.33)

U=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbf{B}_{j}(U)\,h^{-}_{j}\quad\mbox{with convergence in $H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})$.}

An obvious consequence of expansions (
4.30
) and (
4.33
) is that coefficients
\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)
and
\mathbf{B}_{j}(U)
do not depend on the choice of
\rho
as soon as
u
and
U
satisfy the assumptions of Theorem
4.9
.

4.4.
Expansion of transfer operators

Recall the Notation
3.2
for the commutators and the expression (
3.7
) of the transfer operators

(4.34a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=[\Delta_{X},\Phi]\circ\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}\,,

(4.34b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=[\Delta_{x},\varphi]\circ\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\,.

Here we are going to combine these expressions with the expansions (
4.30
) and (
4.33
)

a) Let
u\in\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
, set
\rho=\frac{r_{0}}{2}
. Then the series expansion (
4.30
) of
u
holds in
\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)
. Let
\varepsilon>0
be given. Applying the change of variables
\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}
we deduce from (
4.30
)

(\mathcal{H}_{1/\varepsilon}u)(X)=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,\varepsilon^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}h^{+}_{j}(X)\quad\mbox{with convergence in $H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\frac{\rho}{\varepsilon})})$.}

Applying the operator
[\Delta_{X},\Phi]
on both sides, we find the convergence in
L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(\frac{\rho}{\varepsilon}))
of the series

(4.35a)

\mathcal{M}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]u=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,\varepsilon^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\,.

The support of
[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}
is contained in
\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0})
, which is a subset of
\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}\left(\frac{\rho}{\varepsilon}\right)
when
0<\varepsilon\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4}
because, in that case, the radius
2R_{0}
is smaller than
\frac{\rho}{\varepsilon}=\frac{r_{0}}{2\varepsilon}
. Hence, the series (
4.35a
) converges in
L^{2}({\mathsf{P}})
for
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
.

b) Let
U\in\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
, set now
\rho=2R_{0}
. Then the series expansion (
4.33
) for
U
holds in
\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)
. Applying the change of variables
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
we find for any given
\varepsilon>0

(\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}U)(x)=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbf{B}_{j}(U)\,\varepsilon^{-\lambda^{-}_{j}}h^{-}_{j}(x)\quad\mbox{with convergence in
$H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}(\Gamma\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho\varepsilon)})$.}

Hence with the weighted
L^{2}
space introduced in (
2.7
) the convergence in
L^{2}_{{\mathsf{w}}}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho\varepsilon))
of the series

(4.35b)

\mathcal{M}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]U=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbf{B}_{j}(U)\,\varepsilon^{-\lambda^{-}_{j}}[\Delta_{x},\varphi]h^{-}_{j}\,.

The support of
[\Delta_{x},\varphi]h^{-}_{j}
is contained in
\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(\frac{r_{0}}{2},r_{0})
, which is, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
, a subset of
\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho\varepsilon)
. Whence, finally, the convergence in
L^{2}(\Omega)
for such
\varepsilon
.

In the following Proposition
4.10
and Theorem
4.11
we prove that the series (
4.35a
) and (
4.35b
) converge in
operator norms
.

Proposition 4.10
.

For any integer
j\geq 1
define the operators
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{j}
by

(4.36a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{j}\colon

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\to\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}

\displaystyle u\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}_{j}[u](X):=\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}(X),\quad X\in P

and

(4.36b)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{j}\colon

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}

\displaystyle U\mapsto{\mathcal{B}}_{j}[U](x):=\mathbf{B}_{j}(U)\,[\Delta_{x},\varphi]h^{-}_{j}(x),\quad x\in\Omega\,

with the functionals
\mathbf{c}_{j}
and
\mathbf{B}_{j}
defined in (
4.28
) and (
4.31
), respectively. Then there exist a constant
A>0
such that, for all integer
j\geq 1
, the operator norms
\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}
and
\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}
satisfy

(4.37a)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathcal{C}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})$}}\,}{\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}}

\displaystyle\leq A\left(\frac{4}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}

(4.37b)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathcal{B}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})$}}\,}{\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}}{\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}}{\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}}

\displaystyle\leq A\left(\frac{4}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{-\lambda^{-}_{j}}.

Proof.

a) Let
u\in\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
and
\rho={r_{0}}/{2}
. On one hand, equality (
4.29
) can also be written as

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))$}}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}\,|\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla u\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))$}}^{2}}{\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}{\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}{\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}$}}^{2}}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}}^{2}}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}}^{2}}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}}^{2}},

and on the other hand we have

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}$}}\,}{\|[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\|[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\|[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}}}\leq C_{\Phi}\,\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pth^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0}))$}}\,}{\|h^{+}_{j}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0}))}}{\|h^{+}_{j}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0}))}}{\|h^{+}_{j}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0}))}}

from which we draw for any integer
j\geq 1

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}$}}\,}{\|\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\|\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\|\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}}}\leq C_{\Phi}\,\frac{\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pth^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0}))$}}\,}{\|h^{+}_{j}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0}))}}{\|h^{+}_{j}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0}))}}{\|h^{+}_{j}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0}))}}}{\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))$}}\,}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}}}\;\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}$}}\,}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}}}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}}}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}}}\,.

To bound the
H^{1}
-norm of
h^{+}_{j}
, we use the Poincaré inequality (
4.8
)

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt|X|^{-1}\,h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))$}}^{2}}{\||X|^{-1}\,h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}^{2}}{\||X|^{-1}\,h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}^{2}}{\||X|^{-1}\,h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}^{2}}\leq\mu_{1}^{-1}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))$}}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}^{2}}

which implies that

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pth^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))$}}^{2}}{\|h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}^{2}}{\|h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}^{2}}{\|h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}^{2}}\leq\mu_{1}^{-1}(2R_{0})^{2}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))$}}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}^{2}}

hence

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pth^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle H^{1}({\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0}))$}}\,}{\|h^{+}_{j}\|_{H^{1}({\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0}))}}{\|h^{+}_{j}\|_{H^{1}({\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0}))}}{\|h^{+}_{j}\|_{H^{1}({\mathscr{A}}(R_{0},2R_{0}))}}\leq\sqrt{1+\mu_{1}^{-1}(2R_{0})^{2}}\;\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))$}}\,}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}}\,.

Thus we have

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}$}}\,}{\|\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\|\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\|\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}}}\leq C_{\Phi}\,\sqrt{1+\mu_{1}^{-1}(2R_{0})^{2}}\;\frac{\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))$}}\,}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}}}{\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))$}}\,}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}}}\;\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptu\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}$}}\,}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}}}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}}}{\|u\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}}}\,.

Using formula (
4.27a
), we find immediately

\frac{\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))$}}\,}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0}))}}}{\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{+}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))$}}\,}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}}{\|\nabla h^{+}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2))}}}=\Big(\frac{2R_{0}}{r_{0}/2}\Big)^{\lambda^{+}_{j}+\frac{n}{2}-1}\,,

which ends the proof of estimate (
4.37a
).

b) The proof of the second estimate (
4.37b
) is very similar:
We take
U\in\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
and
\rho=2R_{0}
, and start with equality (
4.32
).
Then we use the Poincaré inequality (
4.8
) at infinity for
h^{-}_{j}

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt|x|^{-1}\,h^{-}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))$}}^{2}}{\||x|^{-1}\,h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}{\||x|^{-1}\,h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}{\||x|^{-1}\,h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}\leq\mu_{1}^{-1}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{-}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))$}}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}

which implies that (note the slight difference here)

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pth^{-}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{0}/2,r_{0}))$}}^{2}}{\|h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{0}/2,r_{0}))}^{2}}{\|h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{0}/2,r_{0}))}^{2}}{\|h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{A}}(r_{0}/2,r_{0}))}^{2}}\leq\mu_{1}^{-1}(r_{0})^{2}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{-}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))$}}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}{\|\nabla h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))}^{2}}\,.

We deduce

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathbf{B}_{j}(U)\,[\Delta_{x},\varphi]h^{-}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}$}}\,}{\|\mathbf{B}_{j}(U)\,[\Delta_{x},\varphi]h^{-}_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}}}{\|\mathbf{B}_{j}(U)\,[\Delta_{x},\varphi]h^{-}_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}}}{\|\mathbf{B}_{j}(U)\,[\Delta_{x},\varphi]h^{-}_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}}}\leq C_{\varphi}\,\sqrt{1+\mu_{1}^{-1}(r_{0})^{2}}\;\frac{\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{-}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))$}}\,}{\|\nabla h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))}}{\|\nabla h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))}}{\|\nabla h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(r_{0}/2))}}}{\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\nabla h^{-}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(2R_{0}))$}}\,}{\|\nabla h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(2R_{0}))}}{\|\nabla h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(2R_{0}))}}{\|\nabla h^{-}_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(2R_{0}))}}}\;\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458ptU\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}$}}\,}{\|U\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\|U\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}}}{\|U\|_{\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}}}\,,

and we conclude using formula (
4.27b
).
∎

Theorem 4.11
.

The transfer operators
\mathcal{M}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}
are given by the operator series

(4.38)

\mathcal{M}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]=\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{\lambda_{j}^{+}}\mathcal{C}_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]=\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{-\lambda_{j}^{-}}\mathcal{B}_{j}\,,

which, for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
, are normally converging in
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})
, respectively:

(4.39)

\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{\lambda_{j}^{+}}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathcal{C}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})$}}\,}{\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}}\!<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{-\lambda_{j}^{-}}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathcal{B}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})$}}\,}{\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}}{\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}}{\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}}\!<\infty,\quad 0\leq\varepsilon<\tfrac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4}\,.

In view of estimates (
4.37a
) and (
4.37b
), the sums in (
4.39
) compare to the pseudo-geometrical series

\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{\lambda_{j}^{+}}\left(\frac{4}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{-\lambda_{j}^{-}}\left(\frac{4}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{-\lambda^{-}_{j}}.

If the
\lambda^{+}_{j}
and
-\lambda^{-}_{j}
behave like the integer
j
, the convergence for
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
is obvious, and this occurs in dimension
n=2
. But for higher dimensions
n
, the distance between consecutive
\lambda^{\pm}_{j}
globally tends to
0
as
j\to\infty
. This is quantified with the help of the spectral counting function (Weyl’s law).

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\#\{\mu_{j}\leq\mu\}
denote the number of eigenvalues (counting multiplicity) of
{\mathfrak{L}}^{\mathrm{dir}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}
that are less than or equal to
\mu
, then we have

\#\{\mu_{j}\leq\mu\}\;\leq\;d_{n}\,\mu^{(n-1)/2},\quad\forall\mu\geq 1,

for some constant
d_{n}>0
that depends only on
n
.

Proof.

If
N_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}(\mu)
is the number of eigenvalues (counting multiplicity) of the (positive) Laplace-Beltrami operator
{\mathfrak{L}}_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}
on
H^{1}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
that are smaller than or equal to
\mu>0
, then we have

N_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}(\mu)\leq\frac{2}{(n-1)!}\,\mu^{(n-1)/2}+c_{n}\mu^{(n-2)/2}+c_{n}

for some constant
c_{n}>0
that depends only on
n
(cf., e.g., Shubin
[
39
, p. 172]
).
Since the
j
-th eigenvalue of
{\mathfrak{L}}_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}
on
H^{1}_{0}(\hat{\Gamma})
is bigger than or equal to the
j
-th eigenvalue of
{\mathfrak{L}}_{{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}}
on
H^{1}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
, we deduce the inequality in the lemma.
∎

Now, in the same spirit, we denote by
\#\{\pm\lambda^{\pm}_{j}\leq\lambda\}
the number of
\pm\lambda^{\pm}_{j}
’s less than or equal to
\lambda
(with repetitions corresponding to multiple eigenvalues
\mu_{j}
). As a consequence of Lemma
4.12
, using formulas (
4.20
) we find:

Corollary 4.13
.

There exists a constant
d^{\prime}_{n}
depending only on
n
such that, for all
\lambda\geq 1
,

(4.40a)

\displaystyle\#\{\lambda^{+}_{j}\leq\lambda\}

\displaystyle\leq d^{\prime}_{n}\,\lambda^{n-1},

(4.40b)

\displaystyle\#\{-\lambda^{-}_{j}\leq\lambda\}

\displaystyle\leq d^{\prime}_{n}\,\lambda^{n-1}.

The proof of normal convergence of the series (
4.38
) then relies on the fact that generalized power series
\sum_{j\geq 1}\xi^{\lambda_{j}}
with exponents
\{\lambda_{j}\}
whose counting function has a polynomial growth is converging for
0\leq\xi<1
, just as a standard series with integer exponents. There is an analogous result in the framework of generalized power series, see Lemma
B.10
, but although the proofs use similar arguments, we cannot just apply that result here, because the sequences
(\pm\lambda_{j}^{\pm})_{j\geq 1}
may involve nontrivial multiplicities.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
\{\lambda_{j}\}
be a sequence of positive exponents satisfying, for some
m>0
, the distribution law

\#\{\lambda_{j}\leq\lambda\}\leq d\,\lambda^{m},\quad\forall\lambda\geq 1.

Then the series
\sum_{j\geq 1}\xi^{\lambda_{j}}
is converging for all
\xi\in[0,1)
.

Proof.

We organize the sum
\sum_{j\geq 1}\xi^{\lambda_{j}}
by packets:

\sum_{j\geq 1}\xi^{\lambda_{j}}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{j,\ \ell\leq\lambda_{j}<\ell+1}\xi^{\lambda_{j}}\Big).

In each packet,
\xi^{\lambda_{j}}\leq\xi^{\ell}
because
0\leq\xi<1
, so this packet
can be bounded by its number of elements multiplied by
\xi^{\ell}
, arriving at

\sum_{j,\ \ell\leq\lambda_{j}<\ell+1}\xi^{\lambda_{j}}\leq\big(\#\{\lambda_{j}\leq\ell+1\}\big)\;\xi^{\ell}\leq d\,(\ell+1)^{m}\xi^{\ell}.

Since the sum
\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}(\ell+1)^{m}\xi^{\ell}
is convergent for
0\leq\xi<1
, the lemma is proved.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.11
.

We only have to prove that the series

\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{\lambda_{j}^{+}}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathcal{C}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})$}}\,}{\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{-\lambda_{j}^{-}}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathcal{B}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})$}}\,}{\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}}{\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}}{\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}}

are converging for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
. Using (
4.37a
)-(
4.37b
), we have:

\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{\lambda_{j}^{+}}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathcal{C}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})$}}\,}{\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathcal{C}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega},\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}})}}\!\leq A\sum_{j\geq 1}\left(\frac{4\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}\!\!\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{-\lambda_{j}^{-}}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathcal{B}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})$}}\,}{\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}}{\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}}{\|\mathcal{B}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega})}}\!\leq A\sum_{j\geq 1}\left(\frac{4\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{-\lambda^{-}_{j}}

hence the convergence using Corollary
4.13
and Lemma
4.14
.
∎

Remark 4.15
.

The factor
4
dividing
\varepsilon_{0}
in the convergence range in
\varepsilon
comes from the choice that we have made for the supports of the cutoff functions
\Phi
and
\varphi
in (
3.1
) and (
3.2
). If, instead, we impose
\Phi\equiv 1
on
{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\varkappa R_{0})
and
\varphi\equiv 1
on
{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/\varkappa)
for some
\varkappa>1
, then the convergence range would be
{\varepsilon_{0}}/{\varkappa^{2}}
, but the constant
A
appearing in (
4.37a
)-(
4.37b
) would blow up as
\varkappa
is closer to
1
.

5.
Inversion by a Neumann series

5.1.
Expansion of the operator
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]

With the converging expansions of
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]
at hand (Theorem
4.11
), we have a converging expansion of the full operator
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
(
1.4
) that we write in the form

{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\,\varepsilon^{\lambda_{j}}

with the exponents
\lambda_{j}
defined as (recall that the
\mu_{j}
are the eigenvalues of
{\mathfrak{L}}^{\mathrm{dir}}_{\hat{\Gamma}}
)

(5.1)

\lambda_{j}:=\begin{cases}\ \lambda^{+}_{j}=1-\frac{n}{2}+\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{n}{2}\right)^{2}+\mu_{j}}&\mbox{if}\;j>0,\\
\ \ 0&\mbox{if}\;j=0,\\
-\lambda^{-}_{j}=n-2+\lambda_{-j}&\mbox{if}\;j<0,\\
\end{cases}

and the operator matrix coefficients
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}
defined as

{\mathcal{M}}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}&0\\
0&{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathcal{M}}_{j}=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}&0\end{pmatrix}&\mbox{if}\;j>0,\\[10.76385pt]
\begin{pmatrix}0&{\mathcal{B}}_{j}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}&\mbox{if}\;j<0.\\
\end{cases}

Each operator
{\mathcal{M}}_{j}
is bounded from
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
to
\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}
and, as a direct consequence of Theorem
4.11
, the series
\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\,\varepsilon^{\lambda_{j}}
converges normally for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
. As
{\mathcal{M}}_{0}
is invertible we may write

(5.2)

{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]=\mathbb{I}+\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\;\varepsilon^{\lambda_{j}}

where the operators
{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}
are bounded from
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
in itself and satisfy the estimates

(5.3)

\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})$}}\,}{\|{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}\leq A\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})$}}\,}{\|{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}\left(\frac{4}{\varepsilon_{0}}\right)^{\lambda_{j}}\,,

cf. (
4.37a
)-(
4.37b
).

The series (
5.2
) fits within the framework of convergent generalized power series, as introduced in Definition
B.9
. However, we need to switch from considering the exponents
\lambda_{j}
as a
sequence
with possible multiplicities (repeated values) to using the corresponding
set
of exponents.

Lemma 5.1
.

(i) Let
{\mathsf{E}}
be the
set
of exponents consisting of all the values of the sequence
\{\lambda_{j}\}
, with
\lambda_{j}
given by (
5.1
):

(5.4)

{\mathsf{E}}:=\{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+},\quad\exists j\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*},\ {\mathsf{e}}=\lambda_{j}\}\,.

Then, for all
{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}
, let
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}
be the operator coefficient defined by

(5.5)

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}:=\sum_{\lambda_{j}={\mathsf{e}}}{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}.

It holds that

(5.6)

{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]=\mathbb{I}+\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}}\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}},

and the series on the right-hand side converges normally in
{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})
for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
.

(ii) Let
{\mathsf{e}}_{*}
be the smallest element of
{\mathsf{E}}
. Then
{\mathsf{e}}_{*}=\lambda_{1}>0
and

(5.7)

\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}}\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}=\varepsilon^{{\mathsf{e}}_{*}}\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}}\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\varepsilon^{{\mathsf{e}}-{\mathsf{e}}_{*}}\,,

where the series on the right-hand side converges normally in
{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})
for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
.

(iii) There exists
\varepsilon_{\star}>0
(with
\varepsilon_{\star}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4}
) such that, for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{\star})
, there holds

(5.8)

\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})$}}\,}{\|\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}<1.

Proof.

To prove statement (i), it suffices to write:

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})$}}\,}{\|\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}\;\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}}\sum_{\lambda_{j}={\mathsf{e}}}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})$}}\,}{\|{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}\;\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}}\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})$}}\,}{\|{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}\;\varepsilon^{\lambda_{j}}

and to note that the last sum converges for
\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
by Theorem
4.11
.

The identity
{\mathsf{e}}_{*}=\lambda_{1}
is a consequence of equality
\lambda_{-1}=n-2+\lambda_{1}
. The normal convergence of the series (
5.7
) is proved as above, with the exponents
\lambda_{j}
replaced with
\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{1}
. Hence, point
(ii)
. Finally, point
(iii)
is an obvious consequence of point
(ii)
.
∎

5.2.
Expansion of the inverse of
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]

Let

\mathfrak{A}[\varepsilon]=\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}}\mathfrak{A}_{\mathsf{e}}\;\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}.

We are ready to invert
\mathbb{I}+\mathfrak{A}[\varepsilon]
:
In view of Lemma
5.1
and Theorem
B.12
, for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{\star})
the Neumann series

(\mathbb{I}+\mathfrak{A}[\varepsilon])^{-1}=\mathbb{I}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(-\mathfrak{A}[\varepsilon])^{k}

is a convergent generalized power series with exponent set
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
defined as the
additive monoid generated by the set

{\mathsf{E}}
in (
5.4
):

(5.9)

{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}:=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}{\mathsf{E}}^{k}\,\cup\{0\}\quad\mbox{with}\quad{\mathsf{E}}^{k}:=\big\{{\mathsf{e}}={\mathsf{e}}_{1}+\cdots+{\mathsf{e}}_{k},\quad{\mathsf{e}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathsf{e}}_{k}\in{\mathsf{E}}\big\}.

Then, comparing with (
B.2
), we see that we have

(\mathbb{I}+\mathfrak{A}[\varepsilon])^{-1}=\mathbb{I}\ +\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}\setminus\{0\}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{e}}\,\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}},\quad\mbox{with}\quad\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{e}}:=\sum_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{k}\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\mathsf{e}}_{1}+\dots+{\mathsf{e}}_{k}={\mathsf{e}}\\
{\mathsf{e}}_{1},\dots,{\mathsf{e}}_{k}\in{\mathsf{E}}\end{subarray}}\!\!\mathfrak{A}_{{\mathsf{e}}_{1}}\dots\mathfrak{A}_{{\mathsf{e}}_{k}}\,,

and the sum
\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}\setminus\{0\}}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{e}}\,\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}
converges normally for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{\star})
:

\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}\setminus\{0\}}\!\!\mathchoice{\|\hskip 0.6458pt\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{e}}\hskip 0.6458pt\|_{\raise-1.80835pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})$}}\,}{\|\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{e}}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{e}}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}{\|\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{e}}\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}}\,\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}<\infty\quad\forall\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{\star}).

As
(\mathbb{I}+\mathfrak{A}[\varepsilon])^{-1}{\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{-1}={\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]^{-1}
, we have obtained the following:

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\varepsilon_{\star}>0
be as in Lemma
5.1
. With the notations introduced above, the following holds:

(i) The operator
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
is invertible for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{\star})
.

(ii) Let
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
be the monoid (
5.9
) generated by the exponents set
{\mathsf{E}}
(
5.4
). Define

(5.10)

{\mathfrak{N}}_{0}:={\mathcal{M}}_{0}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}^{-1}&0\\
0&{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathfrak{N}}_{\mathsf{e}}:=\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{e}}{\mathfrak{N}}_{0},\quad\forall{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}\setminus\{0\}\,.

Then the inverse of
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
can be represented as the sum of the normally convergent generalized series with exponent set
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
and coefficients
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\mathsf{e}}
:

{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]^{-1}=\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}{\mathfrak{N}}_{\mathsf{e}}\,\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}}={\mathfrak{N}}_{0}+\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}\setminus\{0\}}{\mathfrak{N}}_{\mathsf{e}}\,\varepsilon^{\mathsf{e}},

where the series convergence normally in
{{\mathcal{L}}(\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}},\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}})}
for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{\star})
.

(iii) The set
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
is discrete, and
its smallest nonzero element is

{\mathsf{e}}_{*}={\mathsf{e}}_{1}=\lambda_{1}=1-\frac{n}{2}+\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{n}{2}\right)^{2}+\mu_{1}}>0.

Remark 5.3
.

(i)
In our application,
{\mathsf{E}}
is a discrete subset of
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.
As stated in Appendix
B
, a sufficient property at this stage is that
{\mathsf{E}}
is a well-ordered subset of
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(meaning that every non-empty subset of
{\mathsf{E}}
has a smallest element). This property is more general because it allows for bounded increasing subsequences.

(ii)
The operators
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\mathsf{e}}
have finite rank because the operators
{\mathcal{C}}_{j}
(
4.36a
) and
{\mathcal{B}}_{j}
(
4.36b
) have rank one.

Let
\Lambda
denote the set of exponents
\lambda^{+}_{j}
(the exponents corresponding to the singularities of the variational solutions). We then have

{\mathsf{E}}=\Lambda\cup(n-2+\Lambda).

In certain cases where we have an explicit expression for
\Lambda
, we can use it to determine explicit expressions for
{\mathsf{E}}
and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
. In what follows,
S_{\omega}
denotes the plane sector with opening
\omega
, where
\omega\in(0,2\pi]
, and
\alpha
is the quotient
\frac{\pi}{\omega}
.

Example 5.4

(of sets
{\mathsf{E}}
and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
)
.

(i)
When
n=2
and
\Gamma
is the cone
S_{\omega}
, we have
\Lambda=\alpha{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
,
{\mathsf{E}}=\Lambda
, and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}=\alpha{\mathbb{N}}
.

(ii)
When
n>2
and
\Gamma
is a
wedge
with opening angle
\omega
, meaning that
\Gamma={\mathbb{R}}^{n-2}\times S_{\omega}
, we have
\Lambda={\mathbb{N}}+\alpha{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
(cf.
[
14
, §18.C]
). Then,
{\mathsf{E}}=\Lambda
and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}={\mathbb{N}}+\alpha{\mathbb{N}}^{*}\cup\{0\}
. As particular cases: for the
half-space
, for which
\omega=\pi
, we have
{\mathsf{E}}={\mathbb{N}}^{*}
and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}={\mathbb{N}}
, and for the
crack
, where
\omega=2\pi
(note that this is a non-Lipschitz domain), we have
{\mathsf{E}}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{N}}
.

(iii)
When
n=3
and
\Gamma
is a
half-wedge
of opening angle
\omega
, meaning that
\Gamma={\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times S_{\omega}
, we have
\Lambda=1+2{\mathbb{N}}+\alpha{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
(cf.
[
14
, §18.C]
). Thus
{\mathsf{E}}={\mathbb{N}}^{*}+\alpha{\mathbb{N}}^{*}=1+\alpha+{\mathbb{N}}+\alpha{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}={\mathsf{E}}\cup\{0\}
. As a particular case: for the
octant

({\mathbb{R}}_{+})^{3}
, where
\omega=\frac{\pi}{2}
, we have
{\mathsf{E}}=3+{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}={\mathsf{E}}\cup\{0\}
.

(iv)
When
n=3
and
\Gamma
is a
circular cone
with opening angle
\zeta\in(0,\pi)
(note that
\zeta=\frac{\pi}{2}
gives back the half-space, and
\zeta=\pi
is excluded by the non-zero capacity condition (
2.1
)),
\Lambda
consists of the roots
\nu
of the equations

\exists m\in{\mathbb{N}},\quad\mathsf{P}_{\nu}^{m}(\cos\zeta)=0,

where
\mathsf{P}_{\nu}^{m}
denotes the order-
m
associated Legendre function of the first kind (cf.
[
14
, §18.D]
). Then,
{\mathsf{E}}=\Lambda\cup(1+\Lambda)
and
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
is given by the general formula (
5.9
). In contrast with the previous examples, the multiplicity of the elements of

\Lambda
is generically
1
when
\zeta\neq\frac{\pi}{2}
.

Remark 5.5
.

We observe that
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
is contained in
{\mathbb{N}}
when
\Gamma
is a half-space, or when a half-space can be obtained by a finite number of reflections of
\Gamma
(as for a sector or a wedge of opening
\frac{\pi}{k}
, where
k
is an integer
\geq 2
, an octant, etc.). In these cases, the series in powers of
\varepsilon
is a classical power series, and its sum is real-analytic in the open sets where it converges.

6.
Expansion of solutions of the Dirichlet problem in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}

Using the ansatz (
3.5
) for the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
, the problem of solving the Dirichlet problem (
3.4
) has been transformed into the equivalent problem of inverting the block operator matrix
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
, as proven in Theorem
3.3
. Then, in Theorem
5.2
, we inverted
{\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]
by means of a Neumann series that takes the form of a generalized power series with exponents in the discrete monoid
{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
defined in (
5.9
), generated by the set
{\mathsf{E}}
in (
5.4
). In this section, we deduce from these results several representations for
u_{\varepsilon}
. We will present a global representation that depends on the cutoffs
\Phi
and
\varphi
, as well as intrinsic representations, which are independent of
\Phi
and
\varphi
, and are defined either close to or away from the vertex of the cone.

6.1.
Global expansions

By Theorem
5.2
we readily deduce the following:

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
f\in\mathfrak{F}_{\Omega}
and
F\in\mathfrak{F}_{\mathsf{P}}
. For any
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
, let
u_{\varepsilon}
be the solution of problem (
3.4
). Let
\varepsilon_{\star}>0
be as in Lemma
5.1
and let the operator coefficients
{\mathfrak{N}}_{\mathsf{e}}
be defined as in Theorem
5.2
. Then the two series

{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]:=\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}\varepsilon^{{\mathsf{e}}}{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{e}}\;\;\mbox{in}\;\;\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]:=\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}\varepsilon^{{\mathsf{e}}}{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{e}}\;\;\mbox{in}\;\;\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}\,,

with coefficients given, for all
{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}
, by the identity

(6.1)

\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{e}}\\
{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{e}}\end{pmatrix}={\mathfrak{N}}_{\mathsf{e}}\begin{pmatrix}f\\
F\end{pmatrix},

converge normally in
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
and
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
, respectively, for all
\varepsilon\in[0,\varepsilon_{\star})
.
Using these series, we can write the following 2-scale representation of
u_{\varepsilon}
:

(6.2)

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=\Phi\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}\varepsilon^{{\mathsf{e}}}{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{e}}(x)+\varphi(x)\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}\varepsilon^{{\mathsf{e}}}{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{e}}\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\,,

which holds for a.e.
x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
and all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\star})
.
In particular, the first coefficients
u_{0}
and
U_{0}
of each series are the solutions of the
limit boundary value problems

(6.3)

\begin{cases}\ u_{0}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\,,\\
\ \Delta u_{0}=f\,,\end{cases}\quad\mbox{and}\qquad\begin{cases}\ U_{0}\in H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})\,,\\
\ \Delta U_{0}=F\,,\end{cases}

respectively, and, in both series, the next exponents with non-zero coefficients satisfy the inequality
{\mathsf{e}}\geq\lambda_{1}>0
.

Proof.

Using Theorem
5.2
, we introduce
the two series
{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]
and
{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]
for
0\leq\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{\star}
, with terms in
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
and
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
, respectively, defined by

\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]\\
{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]\end{pmatrix}:={\mathcal{M}}[\varepsilon]^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}f\\
F\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}\varepsilon^{{\mathsf{e}}}\,{\mathfrak{N}}_{\mathsf{e}}\begin{pmatrix}f\\
F\end{pmatrix}.

Then, by Theorem
3.3
,
u_{\varepsilon}(x)
coincides with
\Phi\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right){\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]+\varphi(x)\,{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]\!\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)
. This leads to the representation (
6.2
) with coefficients
{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{e}}\in\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
and
{\mathsf{U}}_{\mathsf{e}}\in\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
defined by (
6.1
). The expressions for
u_{0}
and
U_{0}
follow from the expression of
{\mathfrak{N}}_{0}
in (
5.10
).
∎

Remark 6.2
.

The two series
{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]
and
{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]
are not independent of each other. With the exception of their principal terms
u_{0}
and
U_{0}
, either one of them can be eliminated from the representation of
u_{\varepsilon}
by taking the corresponding Schur complement. Indeed, the series
{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]
and
{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]
are, by definition, solutions of the system

\begin{cases}\ {\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}\,{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]+{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]\,{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]=f\,,\\
\ {\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]\,{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]+{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}\,{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]=F\,.\end{cases}

(i)
Then, since
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}
is invertible, we see that

{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]={\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}F-{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]\,{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]=U_{0}-{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]\,{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon],

and for the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of (
3.4
) we obtain the formula:

(6.4)

u_{\varepsilon}(x)=\Phi\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right){\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon](x)+\varphi(x)\left\{U_{0}\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)-\big({\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]\big)\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\right\}\,,

which holds for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{\star}
and a.e.
x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
.

(ii)
Similarly, we can get rid of
{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]
noting that

{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]={\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}^{-1}f-{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}^{-1}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]\,{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]=u_{0}-{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}^{-1}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]\,{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]

and writing

(6.5)

u_{\varepsilon}(x)=\Phi\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\Big\{u_{0}(x)-\left({\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,\Omega}^{-1}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Omega,{\mathsf{P}}}[\varepsilon]\,{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]\right)(x)\Big\}+\varphi(x){\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)

for all
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{\star}
and a.e.
x\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
.

6.2.
Intrinsic expansions

Theorem
6.1
shows an expansion of the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
on the whole of
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, but the form of the expansion depends on the cutoffs
\Phi
and
\varphi
and, in this sense, it is not intrinsic. We will see that analyzing separately the solutions in the two regions
“close to the vertex of the cone”
and
“away from the vertex of the cone”
, we can write expansions that are independent from
\Phi
and
\varphi
.

The region “close to the vertex of the cone” is by definition the region where the geometric perturbation is living, i.e. where
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
coincides with
\varepsilon{\mathsf{P}}
. Recall that
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
coincides with
\varepsilon{\mathsf{P}}
inside
{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0})
(see Remark
2.4

(i)
). Thus we may expect that inside
{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0})
, the solutions
u_{\varepsilon}
can be described by the rapid variables
X=\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\in{\mathsf{P}}
. Paradoxically, it is formula (
6.4
) from which
{\mathsf{U}}[\varepsilon]
is eliminated that will lead to such an
inner expansion
.

The other region “away from the vertex of the cone” is the region of the far field with respect to
\varepsilon
-perturbations, namely the region where
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
coincides with
\Omega
. From Remark
2.4

(i)
, we recall that
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
coincides with
\Omega
outside
{\mathscr{B}}(\varepsilon R_{0})
, so the outer region extends in principle to
\Omega\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\varepsilon R_{0})
, where solutions could be described in the slow variables
x
. Such an
outer expansion
will be obtained through formula (
6.5
) from which
{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]
is eliminated

Before proving the formulas for the inner and outer expansions, we introduce certain
canonical harmonic functions

K^{+}_{j}
and
K^{-}_{j}
satisfying homogeneous Dirichlet conditions on
\partial{\mathsf{P}}
and
\partial\Omega
, respectively. Since these canonical functions
K^{+}_{j}
and
K^{-}_{j}
are not identically
0
, they cannot belong to the variational spaces
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})
and
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
, but to some larger “dual” spaces. Their definition relies on the following lemma.

Lemma 6.3
.

a) Let
h^{+}
be one of the homogeneous harmonic functions
h^{+}_{j}
on
\Gamma
. Then there exists a unique function
K^{+}\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathsf{P}})
satisfying the following three conditions

(6.6a)

\displaystyle\Delta K^{+}

\displaystyle=0\quad\mbox{in}\quad{\mathsf{P}},

(6.6b)

\displaystyle K^{+}

\displaystyle\in H^{1}_{0}({\mathsf{P}}\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})\quad\mbox{for any}\quad\rho>0,

(6.6c)

\displaystyle K^{+}-h^{+}

\displaystyle\in H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}}\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(R_{0})})\,.

Here
H^{1}_{0}({\mathsf{P}}\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(\rho)})
and
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}}\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(R_{0})})
are defined as in Notation
4.1
(ii) and (iii), with
{\mathsf{P}}
replacing
\Gamma
.

b) Let
h^{-}
be any of the homogeneous harmonic functions
h^{-}_{j}
on
\Gamma
. Then there exists a unique function
K^{-}\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
satisfying the following three conditions

(6.7a)

\displaystyle\Delta K^{-}

\displaystyle=0\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega,

(6.7b)

\displaystyle K^{-}

\displaystyle\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})\quad\mbox{for any}\quad\rho>0,

(6.7c)

\displaystyle K^{-}-h^{-}

\displaystyle\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0})})\,,

where
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)})
and
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0})})
are also defined as in Notation
4.1
, but with
\Omega
replacing
\Gamma
.

Let us note that, due to its behavior at infinity,
h^{+}
does not belong to
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}}\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(R_{0})})
. Condition (
6.6c
) means that
K^{+}(X)
is equal to
h^{+}(X)
when
|X|\to\infty
, modulo a correction in
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})
. In particular, it implies that
K^{+}\not\equiv 0
. Similarly,
h^{-}
does not belong to
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0})})
due to its behavior at the vertex, and condition (
6.7c
) means that
K^{-}(x)
is equal to
h^{-}(x)
when
|x|\to 0
, modulo a correction in
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
. Then we also deduce that
K^{-}\not\equiv 0
.

Conditions (
6.6b
) and (
6.7b
) mean that
K^{+}
and
K^{-}
, though not belonging to variational spaces
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})
and
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
, satisfy Dirichlet conditions on
\partial{\mathsf{P}}
and
\partial\Omega
, respectively.

We also note that in condition (
6.6c
), the space
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}}\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(R_{0})})
can be replaced with
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}}\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho_{0})})
for any
\rho_{0}\geq R_{0}
. This is because
h^{+}
belongs, by definition, to
H^{1}_{0}({\mathsf{P}}\cap\overline{{\mathscr{A}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})})
for any
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\geq R_{0}
, and
K^{+}
belongs to the same space by condition (
6.6b
). A similar remark applies as well to
K^{-}
.

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

a) Since
\Delta h^{+}=0
in
\Gamma
, we can verify that the identity
\Delta(\Phi h^{+})=h^{+}\Delta\Phi+2\nabla h^{+}\cdot\nabla\Phi
holds in
{\mathsf{P}}
. Hence,
\Delta(\Phi h^{+})
is a function in
L^{2}({\mathsf{P}})
with compact support, defining an element of the dual space of
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})
. We introduce the corrector
Y^{+}
as the solution of the variational problem

(6.8)

Y^{+}\in H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Delta Y^{+}=\Delta(\Phi h^{+}).

Then we take

(6.9)

K^{+}=\Phi h^{+}-Y^{+}.

By construction,
K^{+}
satisfies all conditions (
6.6
).

Proving the uniqueness of
K^{+}
boils down to showing that if a function
\widetilde{K}^{+}
satisfies conditions (
6.6a
), (
6.6b
), and (
6.6c
) with
h^{+}=0
, then
\widetilde{K}^{+}=0
. This is evident because, in this case, we have
\widetilde{K}^{+}\in H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}}\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(R_{0})})
by (
6.6c
), and
\widetilde{K}^{+}\in H^{1}_{0}({\mathsf{P}}\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(2R_{0})})
by (
6.6b
). Thus,
\widetilde{K}^{+}
is a harmonic function in
H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})
, and it must be identically zero.

b) The proof for
K^{-}
follows the same approach. In this case,
\Delta(\varphi h^{-})
defines a function in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, and we can introduce the corrector
Y^{-}
as the solution of the variational problem

(6.10)

Y^{-}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Delta Y^{-}=\Delta(\varphi h^{-}).

Then, we set

(6.11)

K^{-}=\varphi h^{-}-Y^{-}.

The proof of uniqueness of
K^{-}
follows the same lines as that for
K^{+}
.
∎

Notation 6.4
.

For all
j\geq 1
, we define:

a)
K^{+}_{j}
as the solution
K^{+}
given by Lemma
6.3
when
h^{+}=h^{+}_{j}
. We have

(6.12)

K^{+}_{j}=\Phi h^{+}_{j}-Y^{+}_{j}\,,

with
Y^{+}_{j}\in H^{1}_{{\mathsf{w}},0}({\mathsf{P}})
such that
\Delta Y^{+}_{j}=\Delta(\Phi h^{+}_{j})
.

b)
K^{-}_{j}
as the solution
K^{-}
given by Lemma
6.3
when
h^{-}=h^{-}_{j}
. We have

(6.13)

K^{-}_{j}=\varphi h^{-}_{j}-Y^{-}_{j}\,,

with
Y^{-}_{j}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
such that
\Delta Y^{-}_{j}=\Delta(\varphi h^{-}_{j})
.

Although the correctors
Y^{+}_{j}
and
Y^{-}_{j}
depend on the specific choice of the cutoff functions
\Phi
and
\varphi
, the problems (
6.6
) and (
6.7
), which define
K^{+}_{j}
and
K^{-}_{j}
, are independent of
\Phi
and
\varphi
. Thus,
K^{+}_{j}
and
K^{-}_{j}
also do not depend on the specific choice of the cutoff functions and are, therefore, intrinsic objects of the perturbation domain
{\mathsf{P}}
and the limit domain
\Omega
, respectively. Explicit formulas for
K^{+}_{j}
and
K^{-}_{j}
are available only in specific cases.

Example 6.5
.

a) If

{\mathsf{P}}=\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(R_{0})\,,

which means we are removing a circular hole centered at the vertex of the cone
\Gamma
, then we have

K^{+}_{j}=h^{+}_{j}-R_{0}^{\lambda^{+}_{j}-\lambda^{-}_{j}}h^{-}_{j}\quad\mbox{in}\quad{\mathsf{P}}

for all
j\geq 1
.

b) Similarly, if
\Omega
is the finite sector
\Gamma\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0})
, then

K^{-}_{j}=h^{-}_{j}-r_{0}^{\lambda^{-}_{j}-\lambda^{+}_{j}}h^{+}_{j}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega

for all
j\geq 1
.

In general, although explicit formulas may not be available, we can still obtain converging expansions for
K^{+}_{j}
at infinity and
K^{-}_{j}
at the origin. Specifically, by applying Theorem
4.9
b) to the corrector
Y^{+}_{j}
, we find

(6.14)

K^{+}_{j}=h^{+}_{j}-\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbf{B}_{k}(Y^{+}_{j})\,h^{-}_{k}\quad\mbox{in}\quad{\mathsf{P}}\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(2R_{0}),

and, by applying Theorem
4.9
a) to the corrector
Y^{-}_{j}
, we find

(6.15)

K^{-}_{j}=h^{-}_{j}-\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbf{c}_{k}(Y^{-}_{j})\,h^{+}_{k}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\Omega\cap{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2),

Note that the coefficients
\mathbf{B}_{k}(Y^{+}_{j})
and
\mathbf{c}_{k}(Y^{-}_{j})
are also intrinsic; they do not depend on the choice of cutoff functions
\Phi
and
\varphi
. The sums
\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbf{B}_{k}(Y^{+}_{j})\,h^{-}_{k}
and
\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbf{c}_{k}(Y^{-}_{j})\,h^{+}_{k}
can be considered the
primal singular parts
of
K^{+}_{j}
and
K^{-}_{j}
, respectively. In contrast, the terms
h^{+}_{j}
and
h^{-}_{j}
represent the
dual singular parts
of
K^{+}_{j}
and
K^{-}_{j}
, respectively.

Remark 6.6
.

The functions
K^{+}_{j}
and
K^{-}_{j}
are widely used in the literature for extracting coefficients in corner asymptotics (see, e.g.,
[
34
,
15
,
7
]
). We now explain in detail how this is done for the asymptotics at the vertex of
\Omega
for an element
u\in\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
. Specifically, we will use a duality formula with
K^{-}_{j}
against
\Delta u
. Since, for
u\in\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
, we have
\Delta u=0
in
\Omega\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2)})
, formula (
4.30
) yields the asymptotic expansion

u=\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbf{c}_{k}(u)\,h^{+}_{k}\,,

which convergences in
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2)})
.
Then, provided that
\Omega
is sufficiently regular to apply the divergence theorem, we calculate

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}K^{-}_{j}\,\Delta u\ {\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\varphi h^{-}_{j}\,\Delta u\ {\mathrm{d}}x-\int_{\Omega}Y^{-}_{j}\,\Delta u\ {\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\varphi h^{-}_{j}\,\Delta u\ {\mathrm{d}}x-\int_{\Omega}\Delta Y^{-}_{j}\,u\ {\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\varphi h^{-}_{j}\,\Delta u\ {\mathrm{d}}x-\int_{\Omega}\Delta(\varphi h^{-}_{j})\,u\ {\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)}\varphi h^{-}_{j}\,\Delta u\ {\mathrm{d}}x-\int_{\Omega\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)}\Delta(\varphi h^{-}_{j})\,u\ {\mathrm{d}}x\,,\qquad\forall\rho\in[0,\tfrac{r_{0}}{2}].

Since the only part of the boundary of
\Omega\cap{\mathscr{B}}^{\complement}(\rho)
where non-zero traces can appear is
\rho\hat{\Gamma}
, and on
\rho\hat{\Gamma}
the exterior normal derivative is
-\partial_{r}
while
\varphi\equiv 1
, we deduce that

\int_{\Omega}K^{-}_{j}\,\Delta u\ {\mathrm{d}}x=\int_{\rho\hat{\Gamma}}\partial_{r}h^{-}_{j}\,u-h^{-}_{j}\,\partial_{r}u\ {\mathrm{d}}\sigma\,.

Replacing
u
with the convergent sum
\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbf{c}_{k}(u)\,h^{+}_{k}
and using the orthogonality of the angular functions
\psi_{k}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}K^{-}_{j}\,\Delta u\ {\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\rho\hat{\Gamma}}\partial_{r}h^{-}_{j}\,\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,h^{+}_{j}-h^{-}_{j}\,\partial_{r}(\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,h^{+}_{j})\ {\mathrm{d}}\sigma

\displaystyle=\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\int_{\rho\hat{\Gamma}}\partial_{r}h^{-}_{j}\,\,h^{+}_{j}-h^{-}_{j}\,\partial_{r}\,h^{+}_{j}\ {\mathrm{d}}\sigma

\displaystyle=\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,\rho^{\lambda^{+}_{j}+\lambda^{-}_{j}+n-2}(\lambda^{-}_{j}-\lambda^{+}_{j})=-2\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{n}{2}\right)^{2}+\mu_{j}}\,,

where, for the last equality, we used formula (
4.20
). Hence, the extraction formula

\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)=-\tfrac{1}{2\,\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{n}{2}\right)^{2}+\mu_{j}}}\int_{\Omega}K^{-}_{j}\,\Delta u\ {\mathrm{d}}x\,.

Similarly, for the asymptotics at infinity in
{\mathsf{P}}
of elements
U\in\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{P}}
, we can derive the following extraction formula:

\mathbf{B}_{j}(U)=\tfrac{1}{2\,\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{n}{2}\right)^{2}+\mu_{j}}}\int_{\mathsf{P}}K^{+}_{j}\,\Delta U\ {\mathrm{d}}X\,.

6.3.
Inner expansion

We introduce the inner expansion operator

(6.16)

{\mathcal{I}}_{\Omega,\Omega}[\varepsilon]:\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}\ni u\longmapsto({\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}\Phi)u-{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}\left({\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]u\right)\in H^{1}_{0}((\varepsilon{\mathsf{P}})\cap\overline{{\mathscr{B}}(r_{0}/2)}),

which allows us to compactly write formula (
6.4
) as

(6.17)

u_{\varepsilon}={\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}U_{0}+{\mathcal{I}}_{\Omega,\Omega}[\varepsilon]{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon].

We recall that any function
u
of
\mathfrak{E}_{\Omega}
has an expansion

u=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,h^{+}_{j}

as in (
4.30
), and we wish to show that we have

(6.18)

{\mathcal{I}}_{\Omega,\Omega}[\varepsilon]u={\mathcal{I}}_{\Omega,\Omega}[\varepsilon]\biggl(\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,h^{+}_{j}\biggr)=\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbf{c}_{j}(u)\,{\mathcal{I}}_{\Omega,\Omega}[\varepsilon]h^{+}_{j}\,,

where the last series converges in a suitable sense. This amounts to proving that the operator
{\mathcal{I}}_{\Omega,\Omega}[\varepsilon]
expands into a convergent series of bounded operators. In addition, we will prove that the terms
{\mathcal{I}}_{\Omega,\Omega}[\varepsilon]h^{+}_{j}
only depend on
j
and on the shape of the perturbation
{\mathsf{P}}
, but not on the specific choice of the cutoffs
\Phi
and
\varphi
.

Then, applying (
6.18
) to the expansion

{\mathsf{u}}[\varepsilon]=\sum_{{\mathsf{e}}\in{\mathsf{E}}^{\infty}}\varepsilon^{{\mathsf{e}}}{\mathsf{u}}_{\mathsf{e}}

from Theorem
6.1
, we will derive an expansion of
u_{\varepsilon}
from (
6.17
).

So, we now focus on the terms
{\mathcal{I}}_{\Omega,\Omega}[\varepsilon]h^{+}_{j}
. Recalling that
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},\Omega}[\varepsilon]=[\Delta_{X},\Phi]\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{1/\varepsilon}
, we can write

(6.19)

{\mathcal{I}}_{\Omega,\Omega}[\varepsilon]={\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}\circ\big(\Phi\,\mathbb{I}-{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}[\Delta_{X},\Phi]\big)\circ{\mathcal{H}}_{1/\varepsilon}

where
\Phi\,\mathbb{I}-{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}[\Delta_{X},\Phi]
is a compact notation for the operator
U\mapsto\Phi\,U-{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}([\Delta_{X},\Phi]U)
.

We observe the following meaningful relation:

\Delta_{X}(\Phi\,U-{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}[\Delta_{X},\Phi]U)=\Delta_{X}(\Phi\,U)-[\Delta_{X},\Phi]U=\Phi(\Delta_{X}U)

which implies that if
U
is harmonic in
\Gamma
and satisfies Dirichlet conditions on
\partial\Gamma
, then
(\Phi\,\mathbb{I}-{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}[\Delta_{X},\Phi])U
is harmonic on
{\mathsf{P}}
and satisfies Dirichlet conditions on
\partial{\mathsf{P}}
.

Taking for
U
any of the homogeneous functions
h^{+}_{j}
, which are harmonic in
\Gamma
, we note that for the canonical harmonic functions
K^{+}_{j}
and their correctors
Y^{+}_{j}
, we have

Y^{+}_{j}:={\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}[\Delta_{X},\Phi]h^{+}_{j}\quad\mbox{and}\quad K^{+}_{j}=\big(\Phi\,\mathbb{I}-{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathsf{P}},{\mathsf{P}}}^{-1}[\Delta_{X},\Phi]\big)h^{+}_{j}

(see Notation
6.4
a)). We deduce that

{\mathcal{I}}_{\Omega,\Omega}[\varepsilon]h^{+}_{j}=\varepsilon^{\lambda^{+}_{j}}{\mathcal{H}}_{\varepsilon}K^{+}_{j}\,.

Since the
K^{+}_{j}
are intrinsic objects of
{\mathsf{P}}
, the last equality implies that
{\mathcal{I}}_{\Omega,\Omega}[\varepsilon]h^{+}_{j}
only depends on
j
and
{\mathsf{P}}
.

We now proceed to prove some bounds for the
L^{2}
norms of
K^{+}_{j}
and
\nabla K^{+}_{j}
, which in turn provide us with a bound for the
H^{1}
norm of
